Learning single-index models via harmonic decomposition
Nirmit Joshi, Hugo Koubbi, Theodor Misiakiewicz, Nathan Srebro

Abstract
We study the problem of learning single-index models, where the label y ∈ ℝ y\in\mathbb{R} depends on the input 𝒙 ∈ ℝ d \bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} only through an unknown one-dimensional projection ⟨ 𝒘 ∗ , 𝒙 ⟩ \langle\bm{w}_{*},\bm{x}\rangle . Prior work has shown that under Gaussian inputs, the statistical and computational complexity of recovering 𝒘 ∗ \bm{w}_{*} is governed by the Hermite expansion of the link function. In this paper, we propose a new perspective: we argue that spherical harmonics —rather than Hermite polynomials —provide the natural basis for this problem, as they capture its intrinsic rotational symmetry . Building on this insight, we characterize the complexity of learning single-index models under arbitrary spherically symmetric input distributions. We introduce two families of estimators—based on tensor unfolding and online SGD—that respectively achieve either optimal sample complexity or optimal runtime, and argue that estimators achieving both may not exist in general. When specialized to Gaussian inputs, our theory not only recovers and clarifies existing results but also reveals new phenomena that had previously been overlooked.



1
Introduction

Single-index models (SIMs)—also known as
generalized linear models
—are among the most widely studied models in statistics
[
BC64
,
McC84
,
Ich93
,
DH18
]
. They generalize linear regression by introducing nonlinearity through a one-dimensional projection of the input. Due to their simplicity and flexibility, SIMs have become foundational in both (semi)parametric statistics
[
HS89
,
Li91
,
DJS08
]
and machine learning
[
KS09
,
KKSK11
,
BH25
]
.
In recent years, SIMs have also emerged as prototypical models for exploring several key phenomena in modern high-dimensional learning, including: (1)
Statistical-to-computational gaps
, with close ties to problems such as phase retrieval
[
MM18
]
and tensor PCA
[
DNGL24
]
; (2)
Non-convex optimization
, including multi-phase dynamics
[
TV23
,
AGJ21
,
BMZ24
]
and landscape concentration
[
MBM18
]
; and (3)
Representation learning
in neural networks trained via gradient descent, where SIMs offer a simplified yet informative setting for studying feature learning
[
BBSS22
,
DLS22
,
DKL+23
]
.

Spurred by this growing interest, a recent line of work has investigated the fundamental limits of learning SIMs in high dimensions under Gaussian assumption
[
AGJ21
,
DNGL24
,
DPVLB24
,
BH25
]
. In this setting, referred to as the
Gaussian single-index model
, one observes i.i.d. samples
(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}
, where

(y,{\bm{x}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}:\quad{\bm{x}}\sim{\sf N}({\bm{0}},\mathbf{I}_{d})\quad\text{and}\quad y|{\bm{x}}\sim\rho(\cdot|\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle),

(1)

for an unknown unit vector
{\bm{w}}_{*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and a fixed link distribution
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, modeling the pair
(Y,G)
with
G\sim{\sf N}(0,1)
. Thus, the label
y
depends only on the one-dimensional projection
\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle
of the input. The goal is to recover the latent direction
{\bm{w}}_{*}
from these observations.

In a remarkable work, Damian et al.
[
DPVLB24
]
provided a sharp characterization of the statistical and computational complexity of learning in this model. Their analysis relies on expanding the link distribution
\rho
in the orthonormal basis of Hermite polynomials
\{{\rm He}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
; that is,
\mathbb{E}[{\rm He}_{k}(G){\rm He}_{j}(G)]=\delta_{kj}
for
G\sim{\sf N}(0,1)
. They defined
1
1

1

An earlier notion—the
information exponent
—was proposed in
[
DH18
,
AGJ21
]
. See Appendix
A.3
for discussion.
the
generative exponent
(GE) of
\rho
as

\mathsf{k}_{\star}(\rho)=\argmin\{k\geq 1\;:\;\|\zeta_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}>0\;\;\;\text{where}\;\;\;\zeta_{k}(Y):=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[{\rm He}_{k}(G)|Y]\},

(2)

and showed that the optimal sample size
\mathsf{m}
and runtime
{\mathsf{T}}
for recovering
{\bm{w}}_{*}
from data (
1
) scales as (writing
\mathsf{k}_{\star}=\mathsf{k}_{\star}(\rho)
and assuming
\mathsf{k}_{\star}>1
for simplicity)

\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2}),\qquad{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1}).

(3)

The sample complexity is optimal among statistical query (
{\sf SQ}
) and low-degree polynomial (
\mathsf{LDP}
) algorithms, while the runtime is necessary simply to process
\mathsf{m}
samples in
d
dimensions.

Several works have developed algorithms that progressively closed the gap to these optimal rates. In a seminal contribution, Ben Arous et al.
[
AGJ21
]
analyzed online stochastic gradient descent (SGD) on the single neuron model
{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{x}}\rangle)
, and showed it recovers
{\bm{w}}_{*}
with suboptimal
2
2

2

Throughout,
\tilde{\Theta}_{d}(\cdot)
hides polylogarithmic factors in
d
.

\mathsf{m}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-1})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
. To close this gap, Damian et al.
[
DNGL24
]
proposed a smoothing-based modification of SGD inspired by the tensor PCA literature
[
BCRT20
]
, which locally averages the loss landscape and achieves near-optimal
\mathsf{m}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1})
. Finally, the polylogarithmic factor in sample complexity was removed by Damian et al.
[
DPVLB24
]
via a partial trace estimator—again inspired by tensor PCA
[
HSSS16
]
—which achieves
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1}\log(d))
, thereby matching the sample rate in (
3
).

While these results yield a sharp characterization of learning Gaussian SIMs in high dimensions, several conceptual gaps remain:

Why is the vanilla SGD algorithm suboptimal, with runtime
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
instead of the optimal
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1}
? Why do methods such as landscape smoothing and partial trace estimators---both borrowed from the tensor PCA literature---achieve optimal complexity
3
3

3

Let us emphasize here that these algorithms fail to achieve optimal complexity for (slightly) more general SIMs (see Section
5
). Thus an analogy to tensor PCA is not enough to explain their success in this setting.
? And what role does the Gaussian assumption play in these results?

In this paper, we propose simple—and perhaps surprising—answers to these questions. Our key observation is that the complexity of learning SIMs is governed not by Gaussianity itself, but by the problem’s
rotational symmetry
. Specifically, the family
\{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{w}}:{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\}
consists of all pushforwards under orthogonal transformations of the input, suggesting that optimal algorithms should respect this symmetry—that is, be equivariant under the action of the orthogonal group
{\mathcal{O}}_{d}
.

This symmetry-based perspective naturally leads to
spherical harmonics
—which arise as irreducible representations of
{\mathcal{O}}_{d}
—as the appropriate basis for this problem, instead of Hermite polynomials. Adopting this basis not only clarifies the above questions, but also extends the theory beyond the Gaussian setting to arbitrary spherically symmetric distributions.

1.1
Summary of main results

In this paper, we characterize the sample and computational complexity of learning single-index models under general spherically symmetric input distributions. Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a distribution invariant under orthogonal transformations, i.e.,
{\bm{R}}_{\#}\mu=\mu
for all
{\bm{R}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{d}
. Such distributions admit a polar decomposition
{\bm{x}}=r{\bm{z}}
, where the radius
r=\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}\sim\mu_{r}
is independent of the direction
{\bm{z}}={\bm{x}}/\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}\sim\tau_{d}:={\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
. We define a natural generalization of Gaussian SIMs (
1
), which we call
spherical single-index models
, specified by a joint distribution
\nu_{d}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
:

(Y,R,Z)\sim\nu_{d}:\quad R\sim\mu_{r}\;\;\perp\;\;Z\sim\tau_{d,1}\quad\text{and}\quad Y|(R,Z)\sim\nu_{d}(\cdot|R,Z),

(4)

where
\tau_{d,1}
is the distribution of the first coordinate of
{\bm{z}}\sim\tau_{d}
. Samples are drawn according to:

(y,{\bm{x}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}:\quad{\bm{x}}=(r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mu=\mu_{r}\otimes\tau_{d}\quad\text{and}\quad y|(r,{\bm{z}})\sim\nu_{d}(\cdot|r,\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle),

(5)

for an unknown unit vector
{\bm{w}}_{*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Thus, the label
y
may now depend on both
(r,\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)
, rather than only on the scalar projection
\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle
. Unlike the Gaussian case, the conditional distribution
\nu_{d}(\cdot|R,Z)
is allowed to depend on the ambient dimension
d
: our learning guarantees will hold for fixed
\nu_{d}
, with explicit (up to universal constants), non-asymptotic bounds.

We now summarize our main results on estimating
{\bm{w}}_{*}
from i.i.d. samples drawn from the spherical single-index model (
5
):

Harmonic decomposition and lower bounds.

To characterize the complexity of learning in this setting, we exploit the decomposition of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
into harmonic subspaces:

L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{\infty}V_{d,\ell},\qquad n_{d,\ell}=\dim(V_{d,\ell})=\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell}),

(6)

where
V_{d,\ell}
denotes the space of degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics. For each
\ell\geq 1
, we define the
\ell
-th Gegenbauer coefficient of
\nu_{d}
to be

\xi_{d,\ell}(Y,R):=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y,R],

(7)

where
Q_{\ell}\in V_{d,\ell}
is the (normalized) degree-
\ell
Gegenbauer polynomial in
L^{2}([-1,1],\tau_{d,1})
. We establish the following lower bounds on the sample complexity
\mathsf{m}
and runtime
{\mathsf{T}}
for recovering
{\bm{w}}_{*}
using the low-degree polynomial (
\mathsf{LDP}
) and statistical query (
{\sf SQ}
) frameworks:

\mathsf{m}\gtrsim\left\{d\vee\frac{d^{1/2}}{\|\xi_{d,1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right\}\;\wedge\;\inf_{\ell\geq 2}\;\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\qquad{\mathsf{T}}\gtrsim\left\{d^{2}\vee\frac{d^{3/2}}{\|\xi_{d,1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right\}\;\wedge\;\inf_{\ell\geq 2}\;\frac{d^{\ell}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

(8)

These bounds effectively decouple across irreducible subspaces: each term
\ell\geq 1
in the infimum corresponds to a lower bound for learning spherical SIMs using estimators restricted to the harmonic subspace
V_{d,\ell}
, with matching upper bounds summarized in Table
1
. Note that
\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1
and can decay with
d
: thus, these lower bounds capture the competition between the dimensionality
\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell})
of
V_{d,\ell}
and the ‘signal strength’
\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
it carries about
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
.

Subspace
V_{d,\ell}

Runtime optimal

\ell=2

\mathsf{m}\asymp\frac{d}{\|\xi_{d,2}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\qquad{\mathsf{T}}\asymp\frac{d^{2}}{\|\xi_{d,2}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\log(d).

\ell\geq 3

Harmonic tensor unfolding

\mathsf{m}\asymp\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\quad{\mathsf{T}}\asymp\frac{d^{\ell+1}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\log(d).

Online SGD

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}\asymp\frac{d^{\ell-1}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\quad{\mathsf{T}}\asymp\frac{d^{\ell}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

V_{d,\ell}
, with their sample complexity
\mathsf{m}
and runtime
{\mathsf{T}}
. Here, the notation
\asymp
hides constants that depend on
\ell
and assumptions on
\nu_{d}
. The estimator in the left (resp. right) column matches the optimal sample complexity (resp. optimal runtime) predicted by the
\mathsf{LDP}
(resp.
{\sf SQ}
) lower bound (
8
). See Section
3
for details and formal statements.

Optimal algorithms and trade-offs.

For each harmonic subspace
V_{d,\ell}
, we construct: (1) a sample-optimal
4
4

4

Throughout the paper, we refer to
sample-optimal
as the optimal conjectural sample complexity for polynomial time algorithms, and refer to the
information-theoretic optimal
sample complexity otherwise.
estimator based either on spectral methods for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
(that is also runtime (near-)optimal) or on tensor-unfolding of reproducing harmonic operators for
\ell\geq 3
; and (2) a runtime-optimal estimator based on online SGD for
\ell\geq 3
. Their complexities are summarized in Table
1
.

This leads to a simple strategy for learning spherical SIMs (
5
): identify
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}
(sample-optimal) or
\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
(runtime-optimal) as the degree minimizing the corresponding term in the lower bound (
8
), and apply the matching algorithm from Table
1
. Note that we always have
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}\geq\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
. If
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}\in\{1,2\}
, the spectral algorithm achieves both optimal sample and runtime complexity. If
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}>2
, it remains open whether a single estimator can achieve both
5
5

5

In fact, we show that for
\ell
even, harmonic tensor unfolding achieves both optimal sample and runtime complexity, with a potential additional
\log(d)
factor in sample complexity.
. More generally, one can construct distributions for which
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}\gg\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
, suggesting that
no algorithm can simultaneously achieve optimal sample and runtime complexity in these cases
. This stands in sharp contrast to Gaussian SIMs, where both complexities are always jointly achievable. Thus,

Sample-runtime trade-offs can appear when learning SIMs beyond the Gaussian setting.

The case of Gaussian inputs.

We now specialize our results to the Gaussian single-index model (
1
), where
\mu={\sf N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})
and
\nu_{d}(Y,R,Z)=\rho(Y,R\cdot Z)
with generative exponent
\mathsf{k}_{\star}>1
. This yields a particularly transparent picture:

(1)

The optimal degrees
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
are always either
1
(if
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
is odd) or
2
(if
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
is even), with the spectral algorithm from Table
1
achieving both optimal sample and runtime complexity (
3
). Thus,
for any Gaussian SIMs, optimal algorithms lie in the harmonic subspaces
V_{d,1}
and
V_{d,2}
, corresponding to degree-
1
or
2
spherical harmonics in
{\bm{z}}={\bm{x}}/\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
.

(2)

The SGD algorithm of
[
AGJ21
]
is dominated by the high-frequency harmonics
V_{d,\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
, while smoothing
[
DNGL24
]
reweights the loss landscape toward low-frequency harmonics
V_{d,1}
or
V_{d,2}
. The partial trace estimator
[
DPVLB24
]
explicitly projects onto them.
Both methods achieve optimal complexity by effectively exploiting these low-frequency components.

(3)

We provide an alternative perspective for understanding the suboptimality of SGD: its optimization dynamics remains essentially unchanged when
{\bm{x}}
is replaced by its direction
{\bm{z}}
, implying it does not exploit the radial component
r=\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
. We show that algorithms that ignore
r
must incur a runtime complexity of
\Omega_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
. In this sense, SGD is runtime-optimal among methods that rely solely on the directional component.
To achieve the optimal runtime
\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1})
, one must exploit the radial component—even though it carries no information about
{\bm{w}}_{*}
and asymptotically concentrates around
r/\sqrt{d}\to 1
.

These results are summarized in Table
2
.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
reviews relevant background and introduces key definitions. In Section
3
, we present our general lower and upper bounds for learning spherical single-index models (Table
1
). These results are then specialized to the Gaussian setting in Section
4
, where we recover and refine existing results (Table
2
). In light of these new insights, we revisit algorithms for learning Gaussian SIMs in Section
5
. We conclude in Section
6
with a discussion of future directions. Additional details and proofs are deferred to the appendices.

2
Setting and definitions

Throughout the paper, we assume our link functions
\nu_{d}
are drawn from the following class:

Definition 1

(Spherical link functions)
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}_{d}
denote the set of joint distributions
\nu_{d}
on
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times[-1,1]
, where
\mathcal{Y}
is an arbitrary measurable space, such that the following hold:

(i)

The marginals of
(Y,R,Z)\sim\nu_{d}
satisfy
Z\sim\tau_{d,1}
(the distribution of the first coordinate of
{\bm{z}}\sim\tau_{d}={\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
) and
R\sim\nu_{d,R}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
not concentrated at
0
.

(ii)

Let
\nu_{d,0}=\nu_{d,Y,R}\otimes\tau_{d,1}
be the product of marginals
(Y,R)
and
Z\sim\tau_{d,1}
. Then
\nu_{d}\ll\nu_{d,0}
and the Radon-Nikodym derivative satisfy
\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{d}}{{\rm d}\nu_{d,0}}\in L^{2}(\nu_{d,0})
.

The corresponding spherical single-index model over
(y,{\bm{x}})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
are then given by:

(y,{\bm{x}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}:\quad{\bm{x}}=(r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mu:=\nu_{d,R}\otimes\tau_{d}\quad\text{and}\quad y|(r,{\bm{z}})\sim\nu_{d}(\cdot|r,\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle),

(9)

where
{\bm{x}}=r{\bm{z}}
is the polar decomposition with
r=\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}\sim\nu_{d,R}
and
{\bm{z}}={\bm{x}}/\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}\sim\tau_{d}
.

From
\nu_{d,0}=\nu_{d,Y,R}\otimes\tau_{d,1}
, we define the null model
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}:=\nu_{d,Y,R}\otimes\tau_{d}
, where
(y,r)
is independent of the direction
{\bm{z}}
. Note that assumption
\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{d}}{{\rm d}\nu_{d,0}}\in L^{2}(\nu_{d,0})
is equivalent to
\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0})
. This ensures that the model has ‘enough noise’ in the label and excludes non-robust algorithms that can beat the lower bounds (
3
) in the noise-free setting (e.g., see
[
SZB21
]
).

Remark 2.1
.

Throughout this paper, we assume
\nu_{d}
to be known. This assumption is mild in high dimensions, where the primary challenge lies in recovering
{\bm{w}}_{*}
. When
\nu_{d}
is unknown, one can modify our algorithms and use random nonlinearities. See discussion in Appendix
A.1
.

Harmonic decomposition.

Let
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}):=L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\tau_{d})
denote the space of squared integrable functions on the unit sphere, with inner-product
\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{z}}\sim\tau_{d}}[f({\bm{z}})g({\bm{z}})]
and norm
\|f\|_{L^{2}}:=\langle f,f\rangle_{L^{2}}^{1/2}
. This space admits the orthogonal decomposition (
6
), with
V_{d,\ell}
the subspace of degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics, that is, degree-
\ell
polynomials that are orthogonal (with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}
) to all polynomials with degree less than
\ell
. We refer to Appendix
B
for background on spherical harmonics.

We use this harmonic decomposition to expand the likelihood ratio of the model in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0})
:

\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}(y,{\bm{x}})=1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\xi_{d,\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle),\qquad\xi_{d,\ell}(Y,R):=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y,R],

(10)

where
Q_{\ell}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is the normalized degree-
\ell
Gegenbauer polynomial. We denote
\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}
the
L^{2}
-norm with respect to
\nu_{d}
. The
\chi^{2}
- mutual information of
((Y,R),Z)
under
\nu_{d}
is given by
I_{\chi^{2}}[\nu_{d}]:=\mathsf{D}_{\chi^{2}}[\nu_{d}\|\nu_{d,0}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,.
See Appendix
A.1
for further details.

Lower bounds.

We establish lower bounds within two standard frameworks: statistical query (
{\sf SQ}
) and low-degree polynomials (
\mathsf{LDP}
) algorithms. Our lower bounds will hold for the weaker task of distinguishing the planted model
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
from the null
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
, i.e., the ‘detection’ problem. These lower bounds directly imply lower bounds on our estimation problem.

•

For
{\sf SQ}
algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
, with
q
query calls of tolerance
\tau
, we derive lower bounds on the query complexity
q/\tau^{2}
. Heuristically, this corresponds to a runtime lower bound of
{\mathsf{T}}\geq q/\tau^{2}
, under the standard assumption that each query requires at least
\Omega(1/\tau^{2})
samples to implement. While this connection is informal, it is validated by our matching upper bounds: the actual runtime of our proposed estimators meets these
{\sf SQ}
-based lower bounds, except for a minor discrepancy when
\ell=1
, where a tighter bound
\mathsf{m}d
(the cost of processing
\mathsf{m}
samples in
d
dimensions) applies.

•

For
\mathsf{LDP}
lower bounds on sample complexity, we work in an asymptotic setting
d\to\infty
. In this case, the bounds hold uniformly over sequences
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
, with
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
satisfying the following mild condition (the model is ‘solvable in polynomial time’):

Assumption 1
.

There exists
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the sequence
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
satisfies

{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})=O_{d}(d^{p/2})
.

We refer to Appendix
C
for background on
{\sf SQ}
and
\mathsf{LDP}
algorithms.

3
Learning spherical single-index models

Let
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of spherical SIMs with
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
(Definition
1
). Under mild conditions, the information-theoretic sample complexity for recovering
{\bm{w}}_{*}
is
\Theta_{d}(d)
(see Appendix
H
). However, polynomial-time algorithms may require significantly more samples—that is, the model exhibits a so-called
computational-to-statistical gap
. We introduce the complexity parameters:

{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d}):=\inf_{\ell\geq 1}\;\;\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\qquad\qquad\mathsf{Q}_{\star}(\nu_{d}):=\inf_{\ell\geq 1}\;\;\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},

(11)

where we recall that
n_{d,\ell}=\dim(V_{d,\ell})=\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell})
and
\xi_{d,\ell}
is the
\ell
-th Gegenbauer coefficient of the likelihood ratio (
10
). These quantities govern the sample and query complexity lower bounds within the
\mathsf{LDP}
and
{\sf SQ}
frameworks respectively, as formalized in the following theorem:

Theorem 1

(General lower bounds)
.

Let
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of spherical SIMs with
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
.

(i)

(
{\sf SQ}
runtime lower bound.)
Any algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
that distinguishes between
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
satisfies
q/\tau^{2}\geq\mathsf{Q}_{\star}(\nu_{d})
.

(ii)

(
\mathsf{LDP}
sample lower bound.)
Assume
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
satisfy Assumption
1
. Then, under the low-degree conjecture, no polynomial-time algorithm
can distinguish
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
from
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
unless
\mathsf{m}=\Omega_{d}({\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d}))
.

The proof and detailed statements are provided in Appendix
C
.

Remark 3.1
.

The lower bounds in Theorem
1
are for the detection problem. A detection-recovery gap can appear in this model when the infinum is achieved at
\ell=1
and
\sqrt{d}/\|\xi_{d,1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\ll d
. In this case, the information-theoretic lower bound
\mathsf{m}=\Omega_{d}(d)
is tighter (Appendix
H
). Thus, the informal complexity lower bounds (
8
) stated in the introduction are obtained as follows: the sample complexity lower bound is the maximum of the information-theoretic bound
\Omega(d)
and
{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})
, while the runtime lower bound is the maximum of
\mathsf{Q}_{\star}(\nu_{d})
and
d
times the sample complexity lower bound—that is, the cost of processing this many samples.

Intuitively, these lower bounds decompose the problem into separate subproblems associated with each harmonic subspace
V_{d,\ell}
. Each
\ell\geq 1
in (
11
) corresponds to the complexity of algorithms restricted to degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics in
{\bm{z}}
(see Appendix
C
for further discussion). Below, we introduce matching upper bounds for each subspace
V_{d,\ell}
, summarized earlier in Table
1
. These algorithms are stated with general transformations
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
. For simplicity, we present our learning guarantees below under the following assumption:

Assumption 2
.

For
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
and
\ell\geq 1
, there exist
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa_{\ell}>1
such that
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa_{\ell}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(Y,R)Q_{\ell}(Z)]\geq\kappa_{\ell}^{-1}\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}
.

For Gaussian inputs (next section), Assumption
2
is satisfied with
\kappa_{\ell}
only depending on
\rho
. In the appendices, we state our learning guarantees under weaker assumptions (with possible additional log factors), e.g., using
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}:=\xi_{d,\ell}/\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}
under moment condition on
\xi_{d,\ell}
.

Remark 3.2

(Weak to strong recovery)
.

We further state our results below for the weak recovery task
|\langle\hat{{\bm{w}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4
. Having achieved weak recovery, one can achieve strong recovery
|\langle\hat{{\bm{w}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1-\varepsilon
, with arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, using an additional
\Theta_{d}(d/\varepsilon)
samples (information-theoretic optimal) and
\Theta_{d}(d^{2}/\varepsilon)
runtime—similarly to prior works
[
AGJ21
,
ZPVB23
,
DNGL24
]
—under mild assumptions. See discussion in Appendix
A.1
.

Spectral algorithm:

For
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we present spectral estimators—similar to
[
MM18
,
DPVLB24
]
—that achieve both optimal sample and runtime complexity. Given
\mathsf{m}
samples
(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})
, these estimators are defined as:

\displaystyle\ell=1:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{w}}_{0}=

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\bm{v}}}{\|\hat{\bm{v}}\|_{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathsf{m}}\sum_{i\in[\mathsf{m}]}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y_{i},r_{i})\sqrt{d}\,{\bm{z}}_{i},

(
\mathsf{SP}
-
\mathsf{Alg}
)

\displaystyle\ell=2:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{w}}=

\displaystyle\argmax_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\;{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{M}}{\bm{w}},\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{M}}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathsf{m}}\sum_{i\in[\mathsf{m}]}{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(y_{i},r_{i})\left[d\cdot{\bm{z}}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-\mathbf{I}_{d}\right].

For
\ell=1
, the estimator
\hat{\bm{w}}_{0}
either achieves weak recovery directly or requires boosting as in
[
ZPVB23
,
DNGL24
,
DPVLB24
]
; we leave the description of the full algorithm to Appendix
D
. For
\ell=2
, the leading eigenvector can be efficiently computed in runtime
\Theta_{d}(\mathsf{m}d\log(d))
via power iteration.

Theorem 2

(Spectral algorithm)
.

Let
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
and set
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
as in
2
. There exists
C_{\ell}\geq 0
that only depends on
\ell
such that for any
\delta>0
, the output
\hat{\bm{w}}
of the spectral estimator (

SP

-

Alg

) achieves
|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4
with probability
1-\delta
when

\displaystyle\ell=1:\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\geq

\displaystyle C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{d}{\lVert\xi_{d,1}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)},\quad

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d,

(12)

\displaystyle\ell=2:\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\leq

\displaystyle C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{d}{\lVert\xi_{d,2}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\left(1+\lVert\xi_{d,2}\rVert_{L^{2}}\log^{2}(d/\delta)\right),\quad

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d\log(d).

Furthermore, for
\ell=1
, one can achieve better sample complexity under an additional condition: the boosted estimator achieves
|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4
with probability
1-\delta
when

\ell=1:\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\geq C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\lVert\xi_{d,1}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d.

(13)

The proof and detailed statement of this theorem can be found in Appendix
D
. For
\ell=2
and
\|\xi_{d,2}\|_{L^{2}}=\Omega_{d}(\log(d)^{-2})
, the additional factor
\log^{2}(d)
can be removed by following a similar argument as in

[
MM18
]
.

Online SGD algorithm:

For
\ell\geq 3
, we propose an online SGD algorithm inspired by
[
AGJ21
]
that achieves optimal runtime. We run projected online SGD on the population loss

\min_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}}\left[\left({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)-Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)\right)^{2}\right],

(
\mathsf{SGD}
-
\mathsf{Alg}
)

with a carefully chosen step size
[
AGJ21
,
ZPVB23
]
. The number of samples in this algorithm corresponds to the number of SGD iterations, and the total runtime is
\Theta_{d}(\mathsf{m}d)
—the cost of computing a
d
-dimensional gradient at each iteration.
Details can be found in Appendix
E
.

Theorem 3

(Online SGD algorithm)
.

Let
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
and set
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
as in
2
. There exists
C_{\ell}\geq 0
that only depends on
\ell
such that for any
\delta>0
, the output
\hat{\bm{w}}
of the online SGD estimator (

SGD

-

Alg

) achieves
|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4
with probability
1-\delta
when

\displaystyle\ell\geq 3:\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\geq C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{d^{\ell-1}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,}\log(1/\delta),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d\,.

(14)

The proof of this theorem follows by adapting the arguments in
[
AGJ21
,
ZPVB23
]
and can be found in Appendix
E
.

Harmonic tensor unfolding.

For
\ell\geq 3
, we present a tensor-unfolding algorithm inspired by the seminal work
[
MR14
]
on Tensor PCA, that achieves optimal sample complexity. We introduce a degree-
\ell
harmonic tensor
{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}})\in\text{Sym}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\ell})
(the space of symmetric
\ell
-tensors in
d
-dimensions). It is defined via the degree-
\ell
Gegenbauer polynomial as

Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)=\langle{\bm{w}}^{\otimes\ell},{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}})\rangle,\qquad\text{for all }{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

(15)

We provide an explicit expression of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}
in Appendix
F
. This tensor can be seen as the projection of
{\bm{z}}^{\otimes\ell}
into the space of symmetric, trace-less tensors. In particular, it has the reproducing property

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{z}}}\left[Q_{k}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle){\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}})\right]=\frac{\delta_{\ell k}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{w}}).

(16)

Given
\mathsf{m}
samples
(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
, we compute the empirical tensor

\hat{{\bm{T}}}:=\frac{1}{\mathsf{m}}\sum_{i\in[\mathsf{m}]}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i}){\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}}_{i}).

(17)

By the reproducing property (
16
), the expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
of this tensor is proportional to
{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{w}}_{*})
, which has principal component
{\bm{w}}_{*}^{\otimes\ell}
. To extract this component, we will consider two different estimators. We follow
[
MR14
]
and construct the unfolded matrix of
\hat{\bm{T}}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\otimes(I+J)}
, denoted
\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}(\hat{\bm{T}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{I}\times{d^{J}}}
, with entries

\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}(\hat{\bm{T}})_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{I}),(j_{1},\ldots,j_{J})}=\hat{\bm{T}}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{I},j_{1},\ldots,j_{J}},

where we identify
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{I})
with
1+\sum_{j=1}^{I}(i_{j}-1)d^{j-1}.

For
\ell\geq 3
even, our first estimator computes
{\bm{s}}_{1}(\textbf{Mat}_{\ell/2,\ell/2}(\hat{\bm{T}}))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}}
, the top left singular vector of
\textbf{Mat}_{\ell/2,\ell/2}(\hat{\bm{T}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\ell/2}\times d^{\ell/2}}
via power iteration, and return

\hat{\bm{w}}:=\textbf{Vec}_{\ell/2}\left({\bm{s}}_{1}\left(\textbf{Mat}_{\ell/2,\ell/2}(\hat{\bm{T}})\right)\right),

(
\mathsf{TU}
-
\mathsf{Alg}
-
\mathsf{b}
)

where the mapping
\textbf{Vec}_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
applied to
{\bm{u}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{k}}
returns the top left singular vector of the matrix
{\bm{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d^{k-1}}
with entries
{\bm{U}}_{i_{1},(i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}=u_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})}
.

When
\ell
is odd, however, the above estimator (

TU

-

Alg

-

b

) (now with
I=\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor
and
J=\lceil\ell/2\rceil
) requires
\mathsf{m}\asymp d^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}/\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
samples, which is suboptimal by a factor
d^{1/2}
. This is due to the covariance structure of the Harmonic tensor
{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}})
: a similar problem, with same suboptimality, arises for tensor PCA with symmetric noise
[
MR14
]
(if the noise is not symmetric and all entries are assumed independent, this algorithm achieves the optimal threshold in tensor PCA
[
HSS15
]
).

Here, we modify (

TU

-

Alg

-

b

) by removing the diagonal elements. We set
I=\lfloor(\ell-1)/2\rfloor
and
J=\lceil(\ell+1)/2\rceil
, and introduce the matrix

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{M}}=

\displaystyle\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}(\hat{\bm{T}})\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}(\hat{\bm{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}-\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})^{2}\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}}_{i}))\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}}_{i}))^{\mathsf{T}}

(18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i}){\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{j},r_{j})\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}}_{i}))\textbf{Mat}_{I,J}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}}_{j}))^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{I}\times d^{I}}.

For
\ell\geq 3
, our second estimator computes
{\bm{s}}_{1}(\hat{\bm{M}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{I}}
, the top left singular vector of
\hat{\bm{M}}
via power iteration, and return

\hat{\bm{w}}:=\textbf{Vec}_{I}\left({\bm{s}}_{1}\left(\hat{\bm{M}}\right)\right),

(
\mathsf{TU}
-
\mathsf{Alg}
)

where the mapping
\textbf{Vec}_{I}
is as defined above.

For both estimators (

TU

-

Alg

-

b

) and (

TU

-

Alg

), we show that given enough samples, the leading eigenvector
{\bm{s}}_{1}
is well approximated by
{\bm{w}}_{*}^{\otimes I}
, and the vectorization operation returns a good approximation of
{\bm{w}}_{*}
. The runtime of this algorithm is dominated by the computation of the top eigenvector
{\bm{s}}_{1}(\hat{\bm{M}})
via power iteration, which requires
\Theta_{d}(\mathsf{m}(d^{I}+d^{J})\log(d))
operations.

Theorem 4

(Harmonic tensor unfolding)
.

Let
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
and set
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
as in
2
. There exist
c_{\ell},C_{\ell}\geq 0
that only depend on
\ell
such that the following holds.

(i)

For
\ell\geq 3
even, the output
\hat{\bm{w}}
of the balanced harmonic tensor unfolding algorithm (

TU

-

Alg

-

b

) achieves
|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4
with probability
1-\delta
when

\displaystyle\ell\;\text{even}:\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\geq C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\Big(1+\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}\log(d/\delta)\Big),\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d^{\ell/2}\log(d).

(19)

(ii)

For
\ell\geq 3
, the output
\hat{\bm{w}}
of the harmonic tensor unfolding algorithm (

TU

-

Alg

) achieves
|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4
with probability
1-e^{-d^{c_{\ell}}}
when

\displaystyle\ell\;\text{even}:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\geq C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d^{\ell/2+1}\log(d),

(20)

\displaystyle\ell\;\text{odd}:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\mathsf{m}\geq C_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\mathsf{T}}\geq C_{\ell}\mathsf{m}\cdot d^{\ell/2+1/2}\log(d).

(21)

The proof of this theorem can be found in Appendix
F
.

Remark 3.3
.

A computationally more efficient partial trace estimator was proposed to recover the principal component in symmetric tensor PCA
[
HSSS16
,
DPVLB24
]
. Here, however,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}({\bm{z}})[\mathbf{I}_{d}]
(contraction of two indices) projects onto
{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell-2}({\bm{z}})
and lower order harmonics, which defeats the purpose of our estimator (and could lead to suboptimal performance if
\ell
is the optimal degree).

Optimal algorithms.

We define the sample-optimal and runtime-optimal degrees as

\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\argmin_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}=\argmin_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

(22)

Then choosing the corresponding algorithm associated to degree
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}
(resp.
\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
) achieves the optimal sample (resp. runtime) complexity among
{\sf SQ}
and
\mathsf{LDP}
algorithms. For
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}\geq 3
odd, we leave open the possibility that an algorithm achieves both sample and runtime complexity. More generally, in Appendix
A.1
, we show how to construct distributions with arbitrary large gaps
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}\gg\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
. As discussed in the introduction, this suggest that no single estimator can achieve both optimal complexities in this case, and sample-runtime trade-offs appear.

Specifically, our example proceeds as follows. Let
\mathsf{k}\geq 1
be an arbitrary integer. The label
Y
is a mixture of two single-index models:
Y|R,Z\sim\nu_{1,d}
with probability
d^{-2\mathsf{k}}
and
Y|R,Z\sim\nu_{2,d}
with probability
1-d^{-2\mathsf{k}}
. The two models
\nu_{1,d}
and
\nu_{2,d}
are chosen such that optimal sample complexity is achieved at
\mathsf{l}_{\star,\mathsf{m}}=10\mathsf{k}
(thanks to
\nu_{2,d}
) and optimal runtime is achieved at
\mathsf{l}_{\star,{\mathsf{T}}}=4\mathsf{k}
(thanks to
\nu_{1,d}
). Thus, in this example:

•

Sample-optimal algorithm:
the harmonic tensor unfolding algorithm at
\mathsf{l}_{\star,\mathsf{m}}=10\mathsf{k}
achieves

\mathsf{m}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}\left(d^{5\mathsf{k}}\right),\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}\left(d^{10\mathsf{k}}\right).

•

Runtime-optimal algorithm:
the harmonic tensor unfolding algorithm at
\mathsf{l}_{\star,{\mathsf{T}}}=4\mathsf{k}
achieves

\mathsf{m}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}\left(d^{6\mathsf{k}}\right),\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}\left(d^{8\mathsf{k}}\right).

4
Learning Gaussian single-index models

We now specialize our results to the case of Gaussian single-index model (
1
). Recall that
\nu_{d,R}:=\chi_{d}
and
\nu_{d}(Y|R,Z)=\rho(Y|R\cdot Z)
. We assume below that
\rho
is a fixed link distribution of generative exponent
\mathsf{k}_{\star}>1
(defined in Eq. (
2
)). In particular, the notations
\asymp
and
\lesssim
only hide constants that depend on
\rho
. From
Section

3
, we need to decompose
\rho
into the spherical harmonics basis. Using the decomposition of Hermite polynomials into Gegenbauer polynomials proved in Appendix
B
:

{\rm He}_{k}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\equiv k\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}^{k}\beta_{k,\ell}(\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2})Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle),\qquad\;\;\|\beta_{k,\ell}\|_{L^{2}(\chi_{d})}^{2}=\Theta_{d}(d^{-(k-\ell)/2}),

(23)

we show the following bounds on the
\ell
-th Gegenbauer coefficient
\xi_{d,\ell}
associated to
\rho
:

Lemma 1
.

For all
\ell\leq\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, we have

\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\asymp d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell)/2}\text{ for }\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\textnormal{ mod }2\quad\text{and}\quad\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell+1)/2}\text{ for }\ell\not\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\textnormal{ mod }2\,.

Plugging these estimates in Theorem
1
, we deduce the following complexity lower bounds and associated optimal degrees for learning
\rho
. For sample complexity, we obtain

\mathsf{m}\gtrsim{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})\asymp\inf_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 1\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}\frac{d^{\ell/2}}{d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell)/2}}\asymp\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 1\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2}\asymp d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2}\,,

and any
\ell\leq\mathsf{k}_{\star}
with
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2
is sample optimal (for asymptotic rates). For runtime, we get

{\mathsf{T}}\gtrsim\mathsf{Q}_{\star}(\nu_{d})\asymp\inf_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{d^{\ell}}{\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 1\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}\frac{d^{\ell}}{d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell)/2}}\asymp\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 1\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}d^{(\mathsf{k}_{\star}+\ell)/2}\asymp d^{\lceil(\mathsf{k}_{\star}+1)/2\rceil},

and only
\ell=1
(
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
odd) or
\ell=2
(
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
even) are runtime optimal. Thus, although
\rho
has vanishing projection on degree
\ell<\mathsf{k}_{\star}
harmonic spaces, this is offset by smaller
n_{d,\ell}=\dim(V_{d,\ell})
, and we should always choose
\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}=\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}\in\{1,2\}
(depending on
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
parity). In particular, we can use our general spectral algorithm (

SP

-

Alg

) to learn
\rho
with optimal sample and runtime complexity:

Corollary 1
.

The estimator (

SP

-

Alg

) achieves optimal
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1}\log(d))
.

Estimating using only directional information.

Consider the same Gaussian SIM with link function
\rho
, but suppose we only observe the pair
(y,{\bm{z}})
, i.e., the label and the direction of the input. This defines a new spherical SIM
\nu_{d}
with fixed radius
\nu_{d,R}=\delta_{R=1}
and conditional distribution

\nu_{d}(Y|R,Z)=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{R}}[\rho(Y|\tilde{R}Z)],\quad\text{where}\;\;\tilde{R}\sim\chi_{d}.

For example, this setting corresponds to the common practice in statistics and machine learning of normalizing input vectors to unit norm. We show below that, in Gaussian single-index models, such normalization necessarily leads to a quadratic increase in runtime complexity.

Under this model, the Gegenbauer coefficients of
\nu_{d}
scale as:

Lemma 2
.

For all
\ell\leq\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, we have

\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\asymp d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell)}\text{ for }\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\textnormal{ mod }2\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell+1)}\text{ for }\ell\not\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\textnormal{ mod }2\,.

Similarly as above, it is easy to verify that the sample-optimal degree is now always
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, while the runtime-optimal degrees are
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2
,
\ell\leq\mathsf{k}_{\star}
. The new complexity lower bounds are given by

\mathsf{m}\gtrsim d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2},\qquad{\mathsf{T}}\gtrsim d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}.

Thus, while the sample complexity stays the same, the optimal runtime goes from
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1}
to
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
. Again, we can use our general estimators from Section
3
to match these lower bounds:

Corollary 2
.

For any
3\leq\ell\leq\mathsf{k}_{\star}
,
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod }2
, estimator (

SGD

-

Alg

) achieves
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-1})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
. For
\ell=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, estimator (

TU

-

Alg

) achieves
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}+1}\log(d))
.

The proofs of all the results in this section can be found in Appendix
G
.

5
Revisiting vanilla SGD, landscape smoothing, and partial trace

In light of our results, we revisit the three algorithms for learning Gaussian SIMs mentioned in the introduction
[
AGJ21
,
DNGL24
,
DPVLB24
]
, and reinterpret their behavior through the lens of harmonic decomposition. Below, we state informal observations which aim to build intuition rather than make formal statements. We provide supporting computations in Appendix
A.2
.

Consider a Gaussian single-index model (
1
) with link function
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and generative exponent
\mathsf{k}_{\star}:=\mathsf{k}_{\star}(\rho)
as defined in Eq. (
2
). Throughout, let
{\mathcal{T}}_{*}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a transformation of the label satisfying:

\|{\mathcal{T}}_{*}\|_{L^{2}}=1,\qquad\|{\mathcal{T}}_{*}\|_{\infty}\leq C,\qquad\Gamma_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[{\mathcal{T}}_{*}(Y){\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(G)]\geq\frac{1}{C}\|\zeta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Such transformations always exist (see
[
DPVLB24
, Lemma F.2]
). Informally, one can construct
{\mathcal{T}}_{*}
by truncating
\zeta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}/\|\zeta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}\|_{L^{2}}
(with truncation at large enough value as to approximately preserve the correlation with
{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
).

Online SGD with Hermite neuron.

In a seminal paper, Ben Arous et al.
[
AGJ21
]
studied online SGD on a non-convex loss over
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, with planted signal
{\bm{w}}_{*}
and a
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
-order saddle at the equator
\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle=0
. Adapting their results to the task of learning Gaussian SIMs, their algorithm performs online SGD on the population loss

\min_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}}):=\mathbb{E}_{(y,{\bm{x}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}\left[\Big({\mathcal{T}}_{*}(y)-{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{x}}\rangle)\Big)^{2}\right],

(
HeSGD
)

and succeeds with suboptimal
\mathsf{m}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-1})
samples and
{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
.

Observation 1

(Informal, harmonic structure of the loss)
.

When
|\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\gtrsim d^{-1/2}
, the loss landscape
{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})
is dominated by degree-
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
spherical harmonics. The resulting SGD dynamics behaves similarly to online SGD using
Q_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)
in place of
{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{x}}\rangle)
.

This suggests that algorithm (
HeSGD
) effectively restricts itself to the degree-
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
subspace
V_{d,\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
. As a consequence, we expect its performance to be constrained by the query complexity lower bound
\Omega_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
, and its behavior to be similar to the degree-
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
online SGD estimator (

SGD

-

Alg

) in Section
3
.
We further provide an alternative perspective that highlights the role of the norm
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
of the input data in learning Gaussian SIMs:

Observation 2

(Informal, norm-invariance of dynamics)
.

The SGD dynamics of
[
AGJ21
]
remains essentially the same if the input
{\bm{x}}
is replaced by
\tilde{r}\cdot{\bm{x}}/\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
, where
\tilde{r}\sim\chi_{d}
is sampled independently.

This indicates that the algorithm does not exploit the radial component
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
, and effectively operates on the normalized direction
{\bm{z}}={\bm{x}}/\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
. From our theory (Section
4
), any such estimator incurs a query complexity of
\Omega_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
. In this sense, algorithm (
HeSGD
) is runtime-optimal among methods that ignore radial information.

Landscape smoothing.

To address the suboptimality of (
HeSGD
), Damian et al.
[
DNGL24
]
introduced a landscape smoothing operator that averages the loss on a sphere around each parameter
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
:

\min_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{u}}\sim{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\left[{\mathcal{L}}\left(\frac{{\bm{w}}+\lambda{\bm{u}}}{\|{\bm{w}}+\lambda{\bm{u}}\|_{2}}\right)\right].

(
SmLD
)

This modification achieves near-optimal complexities:
\mathsf{m}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and
{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{\Theta}_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1})
.

Observation 3

(Informal, low-pass filtering effect)
.

Landscape smoothing suppresses high-frequency components of the loss, effectively amplifying lower-degree harmonics. The initial phase of SGD dynamics behaves essentially like optimization over
Q_{1}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)
and
Q_{2}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)
.

Thus, smoothing can be interpreted as projecting the dynamics onto low-degree harmonic components—specifically, the statistics of the spectral algorithm (

SP

-

Alg

) associated to spherical harmonics of degree
\ell\in\{1,2\}
. In this sense, the first phase of the dynamics on (
SmLD
) essentially corresponds to running SGD on the optimal spectral estimator (

SP

-

Alg

).

Partial trace estimator.

In a subsequent work, Damian et al.
[
DPVLB24
]
proposed an estimator based on the partial trace of a Hermite tensor, inspired by techniques from tensor PCA. Their construction begins with the empirical Hermite tensor:

\hat{\bm{T}}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}{\mathcal{T}}_{\star}(y_{i}){\bm{{\rm He}}}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}({\bm{x}}_{i})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k},

(24)

where
{\bm{{\rm He}}}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}({\bm{x}})
denotes the rank-
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
multivariate Hermite tensor, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\star}
is the transformation defined earlier. The expectation
\mathbb{E}[\hat{\bm{T}}]
is proportional to
{\bm{w}}_{*}^{\otimes\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
. To extract this principal component, they compute a
partial trace
of the empirical tensor by contracting
\hat{\bm{T}}
with identity tensors. This results in an empirical vector or matrix, depending on whether
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
is odd or even. The resulting estimator is

\displaystyle\text{$\mathsf{k}_{\star}$ odd:}

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{w}}_{0}=\frac{\hat{\bm{v}}}{\|\hat{\bm{v}}\|_{2}},\;\;

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}{\mathcal{T}}_{\star}(y_{i})P_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\|{\bm{x}}_{i}\|_{2}){\bm{x}}_{i},

(
PrTR
)

\displaystyle\text{$\mathsf{k}_{\star}$ even:}

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{w}}=\argmax_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{M}}{\bm{w}},\;\;

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{M}}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}_{\star}(y_{i})P_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\|{\bm{x}}_{i}\|_{2})\left[{\bm{x}}_{i}{\bm{x}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-\mathbf{I}_{d}\right],

where
P_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
is a univariate polynomial derived from the contraction of the Hermite tensor. In the odd case, a second refinement phase (see Section
D
) is used to boost
\hat{\bm{w}}_{0}
from
d^{-1/4}
to constant correlation with
{\bm{w}}_{*}
. This estimator achieves the optimal sample complexity
\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and (near) optimal runtime
\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2+1}\log(d))
, matching the lower bounds for learning Gaussian SIMs (
3
).

Importantly, estimator (
PrTR
) corresponds precisely to the general spectral estimator (

SP

-

Alg

), associated to the optimal degree
\ell\in\{1,2\}
harmonic subspaces, using

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)={\mathcal{T}}_{\star}(y)P_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(r)\;\;
with
\;\ell=1\;
if
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
is odd,   and
\;\ell=2\;
if
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
is even.

Observation 4

(Informal, lower-frequency projection)
.

Partial trace effectively projects the high-degree Hermite tensor onto lower-degree spherical harmonic subspaces (
\ell=1
or
2
for partial trace over all but
1
or
2
coordinates).

Although our estimator recovers (
PrTR
) in the Gaussian case, we emphasize that its derivation is very different (including constructing the non-linearity
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)
, see Appendix
G
). We construct it directly from rotational invariance and harmonic decomposition, without relying on prior knowledge of tensor PCA, contractions of Hermite tensors, or Gaussian-specific identities. We believe this alternative, transparent derivation of (
PrTR
) highlights the advantages of the harmonic perspective when learning single-index models.

Remark 5.1
.

We further remark that if we normalize the input
{\bm{x}}
and apply landscape smoothing or partial trace algorithms to the data
(y_{i},\sqrt{d}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{i}/\|{\bm{x}}_{i}\|_{2})_{i\in[m]}
, the sample complexity increases to
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-1}
, and these estimators become suboptimal.

Remark 5.2
.

Both landscape smoothing
[
BCRT20
]
and partial trace estimators
[
HSSS16
]
originate in the tensor PCA literature, where a similar gap between ‘local’ and optimal algorithms arises—with
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2}
versus
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-1}
gap in signal strength. It is intriguing to connect the phenomena observed in Gaussian single-index models (Observations
3
and
4
) to analogous behaviors in tensor PCA. We leave this direction for future work.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we introduced a generalization of Gaussian single-index models, which we termed
spherical single-index models
, that allows for arbitrary spherically symmetric input distributions and general dependence of the label on the norm of the input. We provided a sharp characterization of both the statistical and computational complexity of learning in these models. A key insight is that the
{\sf SQ}
and
\mathsf{LDP}
lower bounds decouple across the irreducible subspaces
V_{d,\ell}
of degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics. For each such subspaces, we established two matching estimators: an online SGD algorithm that achieves the optimal runtime among
{\sf SQ}
algorithms, and a harmonic tensor unfolding estimators that achieves the optimal sample complexity among
\mathsf{LDP}
algorithms. The optimal algorithm is then obtained by selecting the degree
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}
or
\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
that minimizes sample or runtime complexity respectively. In general, these may differ—i.e.,
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}\neq\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
—implying that no single estimator can achieve both optimal sample and runtime complexity. We applied this framework to the Gaussian case, recovering and unifying prior results while clarifying the role of the harmonic decomposition in their performance. Below, we discuss two directions for future work.

Multi-index models.

A natural extension is to
multi-index models
, where the label depends on a low-dimensional projection
y\sim\rho(\cdot|{\bm{W}}_{*}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{x}})
, with
{\bm{W}}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times s}
an unknown rank-
s
subspace. Recent work
[
AAM23
,
BBPV23
]
has shown that learning in such models proceeds via a sequential recovery of directions in the signal subspace. Unlike the single-index case—where degree-
1
and
2
spherical harmonics suffice—multi-index models require higher-order harmonics. For instance, the multivariate Hermite monomial
x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{s}=r^{s}z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{s}
is a degree-
\ell
spherical harmonic with no projection onto lower-degree spaces. In such cases, landscape smoothing and partial trace estimators fail, and the harmonic tensor unfolding estimator on
V_{d,s}
becomes necessary. We expect our harmonic framework to extend naturally to the spherical multi-index setting, and leave this direction to future work.

General symmetry groups.

Finally, our lower bounds in Theorem
1
—and the decoupling into irreducible subspaces—hinges on Schur’s orthogonality relations for the action representation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{d}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
. More broadly, the Peter–Weyl theorem ensures that such decompositions exist for any compact group. This suggests that our bounds, and the decoupling into irreducible representations, hold beyond SIMs and
{\mathcal{O}}_{d}
. We hope to explore this broader setting in future work.

Appendix A
Additional discussions and details from the main text

A.1
Discussion on learning spherical SIMs

Likelihood ratio.

In our
Definition

1
, we consider spherical SIMs such that
\nu_{d}\ll\nu_{d,0}
and the associated Radon-Nikodym derivative satisfies
\lVert\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{d}}{{\rm d}\nu_{d,0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\nu_{d,0})}<\infty
. We can expand the likelihood ratio in
L^{2}(\nu_{d,0})
into the Gegenbauer basis:

\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{d}}{{\rm d}\nu_{d,0}}(y,r,z)\overset{L^{2}(\nu_{d,0})}{=}1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\xi_{d,\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}(z)\,,

(25)

where

\xi_{d,\ell}(Y,R):=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\tau_{d,1}}\left[\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{d}}{{\rm d}\nu_{d,0}}(Y,R,Z)Q_{\ell}(Z)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}\left[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y,R\right]\,.

The mutual
\chi^{2}
-mutual information divergence of
((Y,R),Z)
is given by

I_{\chi^{2}}[\nu_{d}]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d,0}}\left[\left(\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{d}}{{\rm d}\nu_{d,0}}\right)^{2}\right]-1=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}\,.

Link function
\nu_{d}
unknown.

When
\nu_{d}
is unknown, similar to
[
DPVLB24
]
, one can still hope to learn the planted direction
{\bm{w}}_{*}
, as long as it is possible to approximate the non-linearity
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)
using random linear combinations of the first few orthogonal functions in a basis of
L^{2}(\nu_{d,Y,R})
. Similar to
[
DPVLB24
, Assumption 4.1]
this requires assuming that the expansion of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
has non-vanishing mass on these first few basis functions of
L^{2}(\nu_{d,Y,R})
. Intuitively, this amounts to a ‘smoothness’ condition on the link function
\nu_{d}
.
We leave this as a direction for future work.

Weak to strong recovery.

In our framework, it is only meaningful to restrict ourselves to the sequence
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
such that
I_{\chi^{2}}[\nu_{d}]
is non-vanishing and there exists a component
\ell\geq 1
(independent of
d
) such that
\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}>c>0
. For example, in Gaussian SIMs with the generative exponent
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, such an
\ell=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
always (both with or without using the norm).
Under this mild assumption, we can carry out the online SGD algorithm similar to the final phase algorithms of
[
AGJ21
,
DNGL24
,
ZPVB23
]
but now on the frequency
Q_{\ell}
. As we have non-vanishing signal
\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert=\Omega_{d}(1)
, we can achieve strong recovery
|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1-\varepsilon
using
O(d/\varepsilon)
samples, and so
O(d^{2}/\varepsilon)
runtime hiding constants in
\ell
.

Spherical SIMs with sample-runtime trade-off.

Below we show how to construct examples of spherical single-index models with
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}>\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}
. We construct
\nu_{d}
such that it is a mixture of two spherical SIMs. Consider
\nu_{d}^{(1)}
and
\nu_{d}^{(2)}
associated to two Gaussian SIMs with generative exponents
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, where we marginalized over the norm (that is,
R=1
). In particular, as shown in
[
DPVLB24
, Theorem 5.1]
, we can choose the Gaussian SIMs to be
y^{(j)}=\sigma_{j}(R\cdot Z)+\tau{\sf N}(0,1)
, with
\|\sigma_{j}\|_{\infty}<\infty
and
\tau
sufficiently large such that
C^{-1}\leq\nu^{(1)}_{d}(y)/\nu_{d}^{(2)}(y)\leq C
.

Assume that
(Y,Z)\sim\nu_{d}
(recall
R=1
here, and we remove it for clarity) is drawn with probability
d^{-\alpha}
from
\nu_{d}^{(1)}
and with probability
1-d^{-\alpha}
from
\nu_{d}^{(2)}
, where
\alpha>0
is a constant chosen later. In this model, we have

\xi_{d,\ell}(Y)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y]=d^{-\alpha}C^{(1)}_{\alpha}(Y)\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}(Y)+C^{(2)}_{\alpha}(Y)\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}(Y),

where

\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}(Y)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}^{(1)}}[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y],\qquad\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}(Y)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}^{(2)}}[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y],

and

C^{(1)}_{\alpha}(Y)=\frac{1}{d^{-\alpha}+(d^{\alpha}-1)\nu_{d}^{(2)}(Y)/\nu_{d}^{(1)}(Y)},\qquad C^{(2)}_{\alpha}(Y)=\frac{1}{d^{-\alpha}\nu_{d}^{(1)}(Y)/\nu_{d}^{(2)}(Y)+1-d^{-\alpha}}.

From our choice of
\nu^{(j)}_{d}
, there exists a constant
C
, such that for
d\geq C
, we have
C^{-1}\leq C^{(1)}_{\alpha}(y),C^{(2)}_{\alpha}(y)\leq C
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
. We deduce that there exist a constant
\tilde{C}
sufficiently large but independent of
d
such that

\displaystyle\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{C}\max(d^{-2\alpha}\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}),

(26)

\displaystyle\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{-1}\max\Big(d^{-2\alpha}\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\tilde{C}^{2}\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\tilde{C}^{2}d^{-2\alpha}\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big),

where the
L^{2}
-norms are with respect to the associated nulls
\nu_{d,0}
,
\nu_{d,0}^{(1)}
, and
\nu_{d,0}^{(2)}
.

Consider
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
a multiple of
10
for simplicity, and set
k_{2}=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
,
k_{1}=2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5
, and
\alpha=\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5
. Using Lemma
2
, we can bound the contributions from
\nu_{d}^{(1)}
and
\nu_{d}^{(2)}
:

•

Consider the contributions of
\nu^{(1)}_{d}
to the sample and runtime complexity:

–

Sample complexity:

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{1},\;\ell\equiv k_{1}[2]:

\displaystyle d^{2\alpha}\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{2k_{1}-\ell/2}=d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5-\ell/2},

(27)

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{1},\;\ell\not\equiv k_{1}[2]:

\displaystyle d^{2\alpha}\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{2k_{1}-\ell/2+1}=d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5-\ell/2+1},

\displaystyle\ell>k_{1}:

\displaystyle d^{2\alpha}\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\gtrsim d^{k_{1}+\ell/2}=d^{2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5+\ell/2}.

Thus the optimal sample complexity is achieved at degree
\ell=k_{1}=2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5
with
d^{3\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5}
lower bound.

–

Runtime complexity:

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{1},\;\ell\equiv k_{1}[2]:

\displaystyle d^{2\alpha}\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{2k_{1}}=d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5},

(28)

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{1},\;\ell\not\equiv k_{1}[2]:

\displaystyle d^{2\alpha}\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{2k_{1}+1}=d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5+1},

\displaystyle\ell>k_{1}:

\displaystyle d^{2\alpha}\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{(1)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\gtrsim d^{k_{1}+\ell}=d^{2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5+\ell}.

Thus the optimal runtime complexity is achieved at degrees
\ell\leq k_{1}=2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5
,
\ell\equiv k_{1}[2]
with
d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5}
lower bound.

•

Consider the contributions of
\nu^{(2)}_{d}
to the sample and runtime complexity:

–

Sample complexity:

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{2},\;\ell\equiv k_{2}[2]:

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{k_{2}-\ell/2}=d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell/2},

(29)

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{2},\;\ell\not\equiv k_{2}[2]:

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{k_{1}-\ell/2+1}=d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}-\ell/2+1},

\displaystyle\ell>k_{2}:

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\gtrsim d^{\ell/2}.

Thus the optimal sample complexity is achieved at degree
\ell=k_{2}=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
with
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2}
lower bound.

–

Runtime complexity:

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{2},\;\ell\equiv k_{2}[2]:

\displaystyle\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{k_{2}}=d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}},

(30)

\displaystyle\ell\leq k_{2},\;\ell\not\equiv k_{2}[2]:

\displaystyle\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\asymp d^{k_{2}+1}=d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}+1},

\displaystyle\ell>k_{2}:

\displaystyle\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{(2)}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\gtrsim d^{\ell}.

Thus the optimal runtime complexity is achieved at degrees
\ell\leq k_{2}=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
,
\ell\equiv k_{2}[2]
with
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}
lower bound.

From the bounds (
26
), the sample or runtime complexity for each
\ell
is the minimum of the two contributions associated to
\nu_{d}^{(1)}
and
\nu_{d}^{(2)}
. We deduce that in this model:

•

Optimal sample complexity is achieved at degree
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, with a matching algorithm that succeeds with
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
samples and
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}})
runtime (thanks to contribution
\nu_{d}^{(2)}
).

•

Optimal runtime is achieved at degrees
\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}=\ell
with
\ell\leq 2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5
and
\ell\equiv 2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5[2]
. For example, choosing
\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}=2\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5
, we have a matching algorithm that succeeds with
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5})
runtime and
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{3\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5})
samples (thanks to contribution
\nu_{d}^{(1)}
).

We conjecture that for this distribution
\nu_{d}
, no algorithm exists that achieves both optimal sample complexity
\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
and optimal runtime complexity
{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{4\mathsf{k}_{\star}/5})
. Further note, that by choosing intermediary degrees
\ell
, one can trade-off sample and runtime complexity.

A.2
Discussion on Gaussian SIM phenomenology

We provide below quick computations to justify the observations in Section
5
.

Observation
1
: harmonic decomposition of the loss.

First, note that

{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})=2-2\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}}[{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle){\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{x}}\rangle)]=2-2\Gamma_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle^{k},

and it is enough to consider the correlation loss.
Let’s decompose the landscape into contributions from the different harmonic subspaces: using the Hermite to Gegenbauer polynomial decomposition in Eq. (
23
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}}[{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle){\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{x}}\rangle)]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\leq k\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}[2]\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star},\ell}(r)^{2}]\mathbb{E}[Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)]

(31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\leq k\\
\ell\equiv k[2]\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star},\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle),

where
\|\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star},\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}/\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{-\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2})
(see Appendix
B
).
For
|\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle|\geq C_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}d^{-1/2}
, the leading contribution in the loss (and its gradient) is
\ell=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
(recall that the leading term in
Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle)
is
\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell/2})\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle^{\ell}
). Informally, this implies that we could have replaced
{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle)
by
Q_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)
in the above loss.

Observation
2
: dynamics with independent norm.

Let’s consider the loss (
31
) when we have independent norms between the input and the signal:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(r\cdot\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle){\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\tilde{r}\cdot\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\leq k\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}[2]\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star},\ell}(r)]^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle),

(32)

where
\mathbb{E}[\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star},\ell}(r)]^{2}/\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{-\mathsf{k}_{\star}+\ell/2})
(see Appendix
B
).
In particular, the leading term
\ell=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
remain the same between Eq. (
32
) and Eq. (
31
). Following the proof in
[
AGJ21
]
(see Section
E
), the dynamics with same hyperparameters behaves similarly between the two losses (
32
) and (
31
).

Observation
3
: low-pass filtering of landscape smoothing.

Again, it is enough to directly consider the correlation term. Let’s decompose

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{u}}\sim\tau_{d}}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}}\left[{\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{x}}\rangle){\rm He}_{\mathsf{k}_{\star}}\left(\frac{{\bm{w}}+\lambda{\bm{u}}}{\|{\bm{w}}+\lambda{\bm{u}}\|_{2}}\cdot{\bm{x}}\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\leq k\\
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}[2]\end{subarray}}m_{\ell}(\lambda)\frac{\|\beta_{\mathsf{k}_{\star},\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle),

where each frequency (
31
) in the original loss
{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})
is now reweighted by

\displaystyle m_{\ell}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{u}}}\left[Q_{\ell}\left(\frac{{\bm{w}}+\lambda{\bm{u}}}{\|{\bm{w}}+\lambda{\bm{u}}\|_{2}}\cdot{\bm{w}}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\tau_{d,1}}\left[Q_{\ell}\left(\frac{1+\lambda Z}{\sqrt{1+2\lambda Z+\lambda^{2}}}\right)\right].

When
\lambda=0
, we indeed have
m_{\ell}(0)=Q_{\ell}(1)/\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}=1
. For
\lambda\gg 1
, we have
m_{\ell}(\lambda)\asymp 1/\lambda^{\ell}
, and as long as
|\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\ll\lambda^{-1}
, the loss (and its gradient) are dominated by frequencies
\ell\in\{1,2\}
.

A.3
Correlation queries and the information exponent

In the main text, we focused on the
generative exponent
introduced by
[
DPVLB24
]
: this notion tightly capture the optimal complexity of learning Gaussian single-index models among Statistical Query and Low-Degree Polynomial algorithms. An earlier notion—the
information exponent
—was proposed in
[
DH18
,
AGJ21
]
. Specifically, for scalar labels
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, the
information exponent (IE)
of
\rho
is defined by

\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)=\argmin\{k\geq 1\;:\;\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[Y{\rm He}_{k}(G)]\neq 0\}.

(33)

This exponent captures the complexity of learning with so-called
correlation statistical query
(
{\sf CSQ}
) algorithms, which only access labels through correlation statistics
y\phi({\bm{x}})
. In other words, using the terminology introduced in Appendix
C.1
, it captures the complexity of
{\mathcal{Q}}
-restricted
{\sf SQ}
algorithms, with

{\mathcal{Q}}={\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf CSQ}}:=\{\phi(y,{\bm{x}})=y\tilde{\phi}(x):\;\tilde{\phi}\text{ measurable function}\}.

We denote
{\sf CSQ}(q,\tau):={\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf CSQ}}
-
{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
this restricted class of
{\sf SQ}
algorithms.

For
{\sf CSQ}
algorithm, Damian et al.
[
DNGL24
]
showed lower bounds within the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-
{\sf SQ}
framework of

\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)/2}),\qquad{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)/2+1}).

(34)

Note however, that only the generative exponent reflects the fundamental hardness of the learning task: indeed, we always have
\mathsf{k}_{\star}(\rho)\leq\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)
, with
\mathsf{k}_{\star}(\rho)
always one or two for all
y
polynomial function of
{\bm{x}}
(while
\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)=k
if
y={\rm He}_{k}(G)
). In the case
\mathsf{k}_{\star}(\rho)<\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)
, the complexity predicted by the information exponent can be improved upon by using non-correlation queries, such as using a non-correlation loss
[
DPVLB24
]
or by reusing samples
[
DTA+24
]
. Nonetheless, IE remains relevant in several natural settings, such as online stochastic gradient descent on the squared or cross-entropy loss.

Below, we discuss how to recover this information exponent from our harmonic framework when considering
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\sf CSQ}
-
{\sf SQ}
algorithms. Introduce the
{\sf CSQ}
query complexity

\mathsf{Q}_{\star}^{\sf CSQ}(\nu_{d})=\min_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi^{{\sf CSQ}}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},

where we defined

\xi^{{\sf CSQ}}_{d,\ell}(Y,R):=Yq_{\star,\ell}(R),\qquad q_{\star,\ell}(R):=\frac{1}{\|Y\|_{L^{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}[YQ_{\ell}(Z)|R].

Adapting the proofs in Appendix
C.1
, we obtain the following query complexity lower bound:

Proposition 1

(
{\sf CSQ}
lower bound)
.

Fix
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
. If an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\sf CSQ}(q,\tau)
succeeds at distinguishing
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
from
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
, then we must have

q/\tau^{2}\geq\mathsf{Q}_{\star}^{\sf CSQ}(\nu_{d}).

(35)

Using the non-linearity
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(Y,R):=Yq_{\star,\ell}(R)
in our algorithms (

SP

-

Alg

), (

SGD

-

Alg

) and (

TU

-

Alg

) described in Section
3
, we can prove the same Theorems
2
,
3
, and
4
with sample complexities replaced by
\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}/\|\xi^{{\sf CSQ}}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
and runtime complexities replaced by
n_{d,\ell}/\|\xi^{{\sf CSQ}}_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
(one simply plug these nonlinearities in the theorems in Appendices
D
,
E
and
F
).

Specializing to the Gaussian case, one recover the exact same result as in Section
4
, but now with
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
(generative exponent) replaced by
\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}
(information exponent) of the Gaussian SIM
\rho
. In particular, for all
\ell\leq\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}
, we have

\|\xi^{{\sf CSQ}}_{d,\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\asymp d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}-\ell)/2}\text{ for }\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}\textnormal{ mod }2\quad\text{and}\quad\|\xi^{{\sf CSQ}}_{d,\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim d^{-(\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}-\ell+1)/2}\text{ for }\ell\not\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}\textnormal{ mod }2.

Similarly to the generative exponent case (and general
{\sf SQ}
), the optimal degrees for learning Gaussian SIMs with
{\sf CSQ}
algorithms are always achieves at
\mathsf{l}_{\mathsf{m},\star}=\mathsf{l}_{{\mathsf{T}},\star}\in\{1,2\}
, with the spectral estimator (

SP

-

Alg

) achieving

\mathsf{m}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)/2}),\qquad{\mathsf{T}}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{I}}(\rho)/2+1}).

Similar results as in Section
4
hold for learning with
{\sf CSQ}
algorithms without using the norm
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}
. We note, however, that here, non-CSQ algorithms can achieve much better performance (attaining the complexity predicted by the generative exponent).

Appendix B
Harmonic analysis on the sphere

In this section, we overview some basic properties of spherical harmonics, Gegenbauer polynomials, and Hermite polynomials. We refer the reader to
[
Sze39
,
Chi11
,
Dai13
]
for additional background. In addition to these classical results, we provide an explicit harmonic decomposition of Hermite polynomials into Gegenbauer polynomials, which we use in our analysis of Gaussian single-index models.

B.1
Spherical harmonics, Gegenbauer and Hermite polynomials

Spherical Harmonics.

Consider the
d
-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:=\{{\bm{z}}:\lVert{\bm{z}}\rVert_{2}=1\}
with uniform probability measure
\tau_{d}\equiv{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, and its associated function space
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}):=L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\tau_{d})
equipped with the inner product:

\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}=\int_{{\bm{z}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f({\bm{z}})g({\bm{z}})\,\tau_{d}({\rm d}{\bm{z}}),\;\;\text{ for any }f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}).

We will denote
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}:=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
and
\|f\|_{L^{2}}=\langle f,f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}^{1/2}
when clear from context.

For
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, consider
\tilde{V}_{d,\ell}
be the space of degree
\ell
homogeneous harmonic polynomials (i.e. homogeneous polynomial
q:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\Delta q(\cdot)\equiv 0
). Let
V_{d,\ell}
be the space of functions by restricting the domain to
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of functions in
\tilde{V}_{d,\ell}
, that is degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. We have the following orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\,=\,\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{\infty}V_{d,\ell}\,.

(36)

The dimension of each subspace is given by:

\dim(V_{d,\ell})=n_{d,\ell}=\frac{2\ell+d-2}{d-2}\binom{d+\ell-3}{\ell}\,.

For each
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we further fix
\{Y_{\ell i}^{(d)}:i\in n_{d,\ell}\}
an orthonormal basis on
V_{d,\ell}
:

\langle Y_{\ell i}^{(d)},Y_{kj}^{(d)}\rangle_{\tau_{d}}=\delta_{\ell k}\delta_{ij}.

Remark B.1
.

If one considers the unitary representation
\rho:{\mathcal{O}}_{d}\to U(L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}))
of the orthogonal group
{\mathcal{O}}_{d}=\{{\bm{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:{\bm{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{R}}=\mathbf{I}_{d}\}
given by

\rho({\bm{R}})\,f({\bm{z}})\,=\,f({\bm{R}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}{\bm{z}}).

The decomposition (
36
) corresponds to the irreducible decomposition of this representation, that is, the decomposition of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
into a direct sum of irreducible representations of
{\mathcal{O}}_{d}
(see
[
Dai13
]
for a detailed treatment on the subject).

Gegenbauer Polynomials.

Let
\tau_{d,1}
denote the marginal distribution of the first coordinate
\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{e}}_{1}\rangle
with
{\bm{z}}\sim\tau_{d}
. We consider the family of Gegenbauer polynomials on
L^{2}([-1,1],\tau_{d,1})
, denoted by
\{Q^{(d)}_{\ell}:\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
, where
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}
is the degree-
\ell
polynomial satisfying

\int_{-1}^{1}Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)Q_{k}^{(d)}(z)\,\tau_{d,1}({\rm d}z)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{e}}_{1}\rangle)Q_{k}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{e}}_{1}\rangle)\,\tau_{d}({\rm d}{\bm{z}})\,=\,\delta_{\ell k}\,.

A relationship between the spherical harmonics and Gegenbauer polynomials is as follows:

Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\rangle)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}Y_{\ell i}^{(d)}({\bm{z}})Y_{\ell i}^{(d)}({\bm{z}}^{\prime}),\text{ for all }{\bm{z}},{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\,.

(37)

Another important relationship is for any
{\bm{w}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
:

\left\langle Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle\cdot,{\bm{w}}\rangle),Q_{k}^{(d)}(\langle\cdot,{\bm{v}}\rangle)\right\rangle_{\tau_{d,1}}=\,\,\frac{\delta_{\ell k}\cdot Q^{(d)}_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{v}}\rangle)}{Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(1)}\,,

(38)

where
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(1)=\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}
as derived in Eq. (
48
) in the next section. We note that the normalization of Gegenbauer polynomials considered here is such that
\lVert Q_{\ell}^{(d)}\rVert_{L^{2}(\tau_{d,1})}=1
holds. Another popular choice is such that the value at
1
evaluates to
1
which we shall explicitly refer by the family of polynomials
\{P_{\ell}^{(d)}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
. The normalizing factor is such that
P_{\ell}^{(d)}(\cdot)=Q^{(d)}_{\ell}(\cdot)/\ \sqrt{{n_{d,\ell}}}
.

The derivative of the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
Gegenbauer polynomial for
\ell\geq 1
can be expressed as

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}z}\,Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)=Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)^{\prime}=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{(d-1)\sqrt{B(d+2,\ell-1)}}Q_{k-1}^{d+2}(z)=C(d,\ell)\,Q_{k-1}^{(d+2)}(z)\,,

(39)

where for a fixed constant
\ell
and growing
d
, we have
C(d,\ell)=\Theta_{d}(\sqrt{d})
. Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\tau_{d})
such that
f
is invariant by the action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\bm{w}}^{\perp}}=\{{\bm{W}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{d}\colon{\bm{W}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}{\bm{w}}={\bm{w}}\}
which is the set of orthogonal matrices which keeps the direction
{\bm{w}}
fixed, i.e.
f
only depends on the projection
\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle
. Then
f
admits the following decomposition

f({\bm{z}})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{\ell}Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle).

(40)

Hermite polynomials.

Consider the probabilist’s Hermite polynomials
\{{\rm He}_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
