, in the normalization that form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma)
, where
\gamma({\rm d}x)=\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
is the standard Gaussian measure.
{\rm He}_{k}
is a polynomial of degree
k
and

\mathbb{E}_{G\sim{\sf N}(0,1)}\left[{\rm He}_{j}(G){\rm He}_{k}(G)\right]=\delta_{jk}\,.

As a consequence, for any
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma)
, we have the following decomposition

g(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mu_{k}(g)\,{\rm He}(x),\quad\mu_{k}(g)=\mathbb{E}_{G\sim{\sf N}(0,1)}[g(G){\rm He}_{k}(G)]\,.

B.2
Harmonic decomposition of Hermite into Gegenbauer polynomials

The Gaussian distribution
{\bm{x}}\sim{\sf N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})
admits the polar decomposition

{\bm{x}}=\lVert{\bm{x}}\rVert_{2}\cdot\frac{{\bm{x}}}{\lVert{\bm{x}}\rVert_{2}},\quad\text{where }\lVert{\bm{x}}\rVert_{2}=:r\sim\chi_{d}\text{ and }\frac{{\bm{x}}}{\lVert{\bm{x}}\rVert_{2}}=:{\bm{z}}\sim{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\,\,\,\text{are independent}.

Therefore,
x_{1}=r\cdot u_{1}
, where
x_{1}\sim{\sf N}(0,1)
and
z_{1}\sim\tau_{d,1}
. In what follows, we denote
x=x_{1}
and
z=z_{1}
for convenience. The Gegenbauer polynomials are an orthonormal basis for
\tau_{d,1}
and Hermite polynomials are (unnormalized) orthogonal basis for
{\sf N}(0,1)
. Our goal is to explicitly express
{\rm He}_{k}(x)={\rm He}_{k}(r\cdot z)
in terms Gegenbauer polynomials
\{Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)\}
, formalized in the following proposition.

Proposition 2

(Decomposing Hermite into Gegenbauer)
.

For any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

{\rm He}_{k}(r\cdot z)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\beta_{k,\ell}(r)Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)\,,

(41)

where
\beta_{k,\ell}(r)=0
if
(\ell>k)
or
(\ell\not\equiv k\mod 2)
, and otherwise

\beta_{k,\ell}(r):=\frac{\sqrt{k!}\sqrt{K(d,\ell)}}{(N!)\,2^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\,r^{\ell+2i}}{\,\prod_{j=0}^{i+\ell-1}(d+2j)}\right)\,,

(42)

where
N=(k-\ell)/2
and
K(d,\ell)\asymp d^{\ell}
as
d\rightarrow\infty
and
\ell
is constant, i.e.
K(d,\ell)=\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell})
.

Essentially, we are decomposing the Hermite basis into the Gegenbauer polynomials, which is the correct basis for the “directional” component
z
, and explicitly computing the coefficients that depend on the radial component
r
. Such relationship is derived by technical algebraic manipulations, and in similar spirit to
[
LCMSMS23
]
, relating Hermite and Gegenbauer polynomials. However, the precise expression is sensitive to the normalization used for Gegenbauer polynomials. Thus, we provide an explicit calculation of this decomposition in Section
B.3
.

For our upper and lower bound analyses, in order to measure correlation of
{\rm He}_{k}(r\cdot z)
with
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)
, using both type of queries (with or without norm), the asymptotic bounds on the following moments of these coefficients will play an important role.

Lemma 3
.

For any fixed
\ell\leq k\in{\mathbb{N}}
with same parity, i.e.
k\equiv\ell\mod 2
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{r\sim\chi_{d}}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)^{2}]\asymp d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{E}_{r\sim\chi_{d}}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)]^{2}\asymp d^{-(k-\ell)}\,.

In order to show this lemma, we will use the following well-known facts.

Fact 1

(Moments of
\chi_{d}
distribution)
.

For any
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the even and odd moments of
\chi_{d}
distribution are given by,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{r\sim\chi_{d}}[r^{2p}]=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{p-1}(d+2j),

(43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{r\sim\chi_{d}}[r^{2p+1}]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r]\prod_{j=0}^{p-1}(d+2j+1)=\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\Gamma(\frac{d+1}{2})}{\Gamma(d/2)}\prod_{j=0}^{p-1}(d+2j+1).

Therefore, for any fixed
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the asymptotic behavior of the
m^{\mathrm{th}}
moment as
d\rightarrow\infty
is given by

\mathbb{E}_{r\sim\chi_{d}}[r^{m}]\asymp\left(\sqrt{d}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{m\equiv 1\textnormal{ mod }2\}\right)\prod_{j=0}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor-1}(d+2j)\,.

Fact 2
.

For any univariate polynomial
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
forward finite difference of
g
at any value
u
is given by

\Delta^{n}g(u):=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}(-1)^{n-i}g(u+i)\,.

For any polynomial
g
, the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
forward finite difference
\Delta^{n}g(u)\equiv 0
if
\deg(g)<n
and
\Delta^{n}g(u)
is a non-zero constant if
\deg(g)=n
. Moreover, for any polynomial given by shifted binomial coefficient of degree
n
, with shift
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}

g_{n}(u)=\frac{(u+u_{0})(u+u_{0}-1)\cdots(u+u_{0}-n+1)}{n!}=:\binom{u+u_{0}}{n}\,,

the constant value of
n^{\mathrm{th}}
forward finite difference is unity, i.e.
\Delta^{n}g(u)=1
.

Proof of
Lemma

3
.

We will use the above facts directly along with
K(d,\ell)=d^{\ell}
and
N,k,\ell
are constants (not dependent on
d
) from
Proposition

2
throughout the proof. We start by the first part of the claim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{r\sim\chi_{d}}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)^{2}]=\frac{(k!)}{(N!)^{2}\,2^{2N}}K(d,\ell)\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{m=0}^{N}\frac{\binom{N}{n}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{2N-n-m}\,\mathbb{E}[r^{2\ell+2n+2m}]}{\,\prod_{j=0}^{n+\ell-1}(d+2j)\prod_{j=0}^{m+\ell-1}(d+2j)}\right)

\displaystyle\asymp d^{\ell}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{m=0}^{N}\frac{\binom{N}{n}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{2N-n-m}\,\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+m+n-1}(d+2j)}{\,\prod_{j=0}^{n+\ell-1}(d+2j)\prod_{j=0}^{m+\ell-1}(d+2j)}\right)

(using
1
)

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{d^{\ell}}{\prod_{j=0}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{m=0}^{N}\binom{N}{n}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{2N-n-m}\,\prod_{j=m+\ell}^{\ell+m+n-1}(d+2j)\prod_{j=n+\ell}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)\right)

\displaystyle\asymp d^{-N}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{m=0}^{N}\binom{N}{n}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{2N-n-m}\,\prod_{j=m+\ell}^{\ell+m+n-1}(d+2j)\prod_{j=n+\ell}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)\right)

\displaystyle=d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\binom{N}{n}(-1)^{N-n}\prod_{j=n+\ell}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)\,\left(\sum_{m=0}^{N}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{N-m}\prod_{j=m+\ell}^{\ell+m+n-1}(d+2j)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\binom{N}{n}(-1)^{N-n}\prod_{j=n+\ell}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)\,\left(\sum_{m=0}^{N}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{N-m}\binom{\frac{d}{2}+m+n-1}{n}2^{n}n!\right)\right).

We are now going to show that term inside the parenthesis is some constant independent of
d
. A priori it may seem that it depends on
d
, however, we have a sum with alternative positive and negative signs and we will show that all the terms that depend on
d
mutually cancel out.

To show this we first observe that
g(m)=\binom{\frac{d}{2}+m+n-1}{n}
is a polynomial of degree
n
given by binomial coefficient. And, therefore, the
N^{\mathrm{th}}
forward finite difference
\Delta^{N}g(m)\equiv 0
for any
n<N
, or simply
1
for
n=N
using
2
. Formally,

\sum_{m=0}^{N}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{N-m}\binom{\frac{d}{2}+m+n-1}{n}2^{n}n!=2^{n}\,n!\,\cdot\delta_{Nn}\,,

where the scaling factor of
2^{n}n!
of the polynomial
g(m)
can be taken out as the forward finite difference operator
\Delta^{N}
is linear. We conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)^{2}]

\displaystyle\asymp d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\binom{N}{n}(-1)^{N-n}\prod_{j=n+\ell}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)\,\left(\sum_{m=0}^{N}\binom{N}{m}(-1)^{N-m}\binom{\frac{d}{2}+m+n-1}{n}2^{n}n!\right)\right)

\displaystyle=d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N}\binom{N}{n}(-1)^{N-n}\prod_{j=n+\ell}^{N+\ell-1}(d+2j)\,\,\cdot\,\,\delta_{Nn}\,\,\cdot 2^{n}n!\right)=2^{N}N!\,\,d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}

\displaystyle\asymp d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\,.

We now show the second part that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)]^{2}\asymp d^{-(k-\ell)}\,.

Let us consider any
k,\ell
such that they have the same parity
k\equiv\ell\mod 2
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)]=\frac{\sqrt{(k!)\,K(d,\ell)}}{(N!)\,2^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\,\mathbb{E}[r^{\ell+2i}]}{\,\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+i-1}(d+2j)}\right)\,

\displaystyle\asymp d^{\ell/2}\left(\sqrt{d}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{\ell\equiv 1\text{ mod }2\}\right)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor+i-1}(d+2j)}{\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+i-1}(d+2j)}\right)

(using
1
)

\displaystyle\asymp d^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\frac{1}{\prod_{j=\ell/2+i}^{\ell+i-1}(d+2j)}\right)

\displaystyle\asymp d^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\frac{\prod_{j=i}^{N+i-1}(d+2j)}{\prod_{j=i}^{N+i-1}(d+2j)\prod_{j=\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor+i}^{\ell+i-1}(d+2j)}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{d^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}}{d^{\lceil k/2\rceil}}\sum_{i=0}^{N}(1+o_{d}(1))\,\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\prod_{j=i}^{N+i-1}(d+2j).

In the last line, we used the fact that
k\equiv\ell\mod 2
and
N=(k-\ell)/2
and thus for every
0\leq i\leq N
,

\displaystyle\prod_{j=i}^{N+i-1}(d+2j)\prod_{j=\ell/2+i}^{i+\ell-1}(d+2j)

\displaystyle=d^{N+\ell/2}\prod_{j=i}^{N+i-1}\left(1+\frac{2j}{d}\right)\prod_{j=\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor+i}^{\ell+i-1}\left(1+\frac{2j}{d}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+o_{d}(1))\,d^{N+\ell-\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\,,

and

N+\ell-\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor=\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}+\ell-\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor=\frac{k+\ell}{2}-\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor=\lceil k/2\rceil\,.

Continuing to simplify the original expression

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)]

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{d^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}}{d^{\lceil k/2\rceil}}\sum_{i=0}^{N}(1+o_{d}(1))\,\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\prod_{j=i}^{N+i-1}(d+2j)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}}\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{N}{i}(-1)^{N-i}\binom{\frac{d}{2}+i+N-1}{N}2^{N}N!

\displaystyle=d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}2^{N}N!\,\,\asymp\,\,d^{-\frac{(k-\ell)}{2}}\,.

Here the last line followed from the fact that the polynomial
g(i)=\binom{\frac{d}{2}+i+N-1}{N}
is of degree
N
given by a shifted binomial coefficient, and thus, the
N^{\mathrm{th}}
forward finite difference of
g
is constant, which in this case is just
1
by
2
. We finally conclude the proof by noting that
\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k,\ell}(r)]^{2}\asymp d^{-(k-\ell)}
.
∎

B.3
Proof of
Proposition

2

We now return to the deferred proof of
Proposition

2
. First, we use the explicit expression of
{\rm He}_{k}
so that
\lVert{\rm He}_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}}=1
.

{\rm He}_{k}(r\cdot z)={\rm He}_{k}(x)=\sqrt{k!}\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m!(k-2m)!}\frac{x^{k-2m}}{2^{m}}=\sqrt{k!}\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{m}r^{k-2m}}{m!(k-2m)!}\frac{z^{k-2m}}{2^{m}}\,.

(44)

Our goal is to express
z^{k-2m}
in terms of
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)
to get the final decomposition. To this end, we use the explicit expressions computed with (a different normalization) of Gegenbauer polynomials. In particular, using
[
DLMF
,
Eq. 18.18.17
]

z^{n}=\frac{n!}{2^{n}}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{\alpha+n-2l}{\alpha}\frac{1}{l!(\alpha+1)_{n-l}}C^{(\alpha)}_{n-2l}(z)\,,

(45)

where
(b)_{a}
is the rising factorial and
C^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}(z)
is an unnormalized Gegenbauer polynomial
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)
with
\alpha=(d-2)/2\,
satisfying

\int_{-1}^{+1}C_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}(z)C_{k}^{(\alpha)}(z)(1-z^{2})^{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}{\rm d}z=\delta_{\ell k}\,\frac{\pi 2^{1-2\alpha}\Gamma(\ell+2\alpha)}{\ell!(\ell+\alpha)[\Gamma(\alpha)]^{2}}

(46)

We can express
C_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}(z)/\sqrt{K(d,\ell)}=\,Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(z)
where
K(d,\ell)
can be computed using

\displaystyle 1=\frac{1}{K(d,\ell)}\int_{-1}^{+1}C_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}(z)^{2}\,\tau_{d,1}({\rm d}z)=\frac{1}{\scriptstyle{K(d,\ell)\mathsf{B}\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}}\int_{-1}^{+1}C_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}(z)^{2}\,(1-z^{2})^{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}\tau_{d,1}({\rm d}z)\,

where
\mathsf{B}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the standard Beta function. We can use Eq. (
46
) to compute

K(d,\ell)=\frac{\pi 2^{1-2\alpha}\Gamma(\ell+2\alpha)}{\mathsf{B}\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\ell!(\ell+\alpha)[\Gamma(\alpha)]^{2}}

(47)

It is straight-forward to simplify

K(d,\ell)=\frac{\alpha\Gamma(\ell+2\alpha)}{\ell!\,(\alpha+\ell)\Gamma(2\alpha)}=\frac{(d-2)\Gamma(d-2+\ell)}{\ell!\,(d-2+2\ell)\Gamma(d-2)}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell})\,,

and also substituting
C_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}(1)
from
[
Wik24
]
, we obtain

Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(1)=\frac{C^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}(1)}{\sqrt{K(d,\ell)}}=\sqrt{\frac{\ell!\,(\alpha+\ell)\Gamma(2\alpha)}{\alpha\Gamma(2\alpha+\ell)}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(2\alpha+\ell)}{\Gamma(2\alpha)\ell!}=\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+d-2}{d-2}\binom{d+\ell-3}{\ell}}=\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}\,.

(48)

We are now ready to combine the equations derived and compute the desired decomposition Eq. (
41
). For any
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let
\boxed{M}=\{m\in{\mathbb{N}}:m\leq M\text{ and }m\equiv M\mod 2\}
. Recall from (
44
)

\displaystyle{\rm He}_{k}(r\cdot u)=\sqrt{k!}\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{m}r^{k-2m}}{m!(k-2m)!}\frac{u^{k-2m}}{2^{m}}=\sqrt{k!}\sum_{m\in\boxed{k}}\frac{(-1)^{(k-m)/2}r^{m}}{m!((k-m)/2)!}\frac{u^{m}}{2^{(k-m)/2}}

(Change of variables)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{k!}\sum_{m\in\boxed{k}}\frac{(-1)^{(k-m)/2}r^{m}}{m!((k-m)/2)!}\frac{m!}{2^{m}2^{(k-m)/2}}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\frac{\alpha+m-2l}{\alpha}\frac{1}{l!(\alpha+1)_{m-l}}C^{(\alpha)}_{m-2l}(u)

(Using (
45
))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{k!}\sum_{m\in\boxed{k}}\frac{(-1)^{(k-m)/2}r^{m}}{((k-m)/2)!}\frac{1}{2^{(k+m)/2}}\sum_{\ell\in\boxed{m}}\frac{\alpha+\ell}{\alpha}\frac{1}{((m-\ell)/2)!(\alpha+1)_{(m+\ell)/2}}\sqrt{K(d,\ell)}\,Q^{(d)}_{\ell}(u)

(Changing
\ell=m-2l
and
C^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}=\sqrt{K(d,\ell)}\,Q^{(d)}_{\ell}
)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(k!)\,K(d,\ell)}\sum_{\ell\in\boxed{k}}Q^{(d)}_{\ell}(u)\frac{\alpha+\ell}{\alpha}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=\ell\\
m\equiv k\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{(k-m)/2}r^{m}}{((k-m)/2)!}\frac{1}{2^{(k+m)/2}((m-\ell)/2)!(\alpha+1)_{(m+\ell)/2}}\right)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(u)\beta_{k,\ell}(r)\,,\text{ where if $\ell\not\in\boxed{k}$ then $\beta_{k,\ell}(r)=0$.}

Otherwise, letting
N=(k-\ell)/2

\displaystyle\beta_{k,\ell}(r)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{k!\,K(d,\ell)}\frac{\alpha+\ell}{\alpha}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=\ell\\
m\equiv k\text{ mod }2\end{subarray}}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{(k-m)/2}r^{m}}{((k-m)/2)!\,2^{(k+m)/2}((m-\ell)/2)!(\alpha+1)_{(m+\ell)/2}}\right)\,

\displaystyle=\sqrt{k!\,K(d,\ell)}\frac{\alpha+\ell}{\alpha}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{N-i}r^{\ell+2i}}{(N-i)!\,2^{(k+\ell)/2+i}(i)!(\alpha+1)_{\ell+i}}\right)\,

(changing
m=\ell+2i
)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{k!\,K(d,\ell)}}{(N!)\,2^{N}}\frac{\alpha+\ell}{\alpha}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{N}{i}\frac{(-1)^{N-i}r^{\ell+2i}}{2^{\ell+i}(\alpha+1)_{\ell+i}}\right)\,.

Recall that
\alpha=(d-2)/2
here, and thus

2^{\ell+i}(\alpha+1)_{\ell+i}=2^{\ell+i}\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+i-1}(\alpha+1+j)=2^{\ell+i}\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+i-1}\left(\frac{d-2}{2}+1+j\right)=\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+i-1}\left(d+2j\right)\,.

Thus, for
\ell\equiv k\mod 2

\displaystyle\beta_{k,\ell}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{k!\,K(d,\ell)}}{(N!)\,2^{N}}\frac{d+2\ell-2}{d-2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{N}{i}\frac{(-1)^{N-i}r^{\ell+2i}}{\prod_{j=0}^{\ell+i-1}(d+2j)}\right)\,.

The lemma follows by redefining
K(d,\ell)
with
K(d,\ell)(d+2\ell-2)^{2}/(d-2)^{2}=\Theta_{d}(d^{\ell})\,.

Appendix C
Statistical Query (SQ) and Low-Degree Polynomial (LDP) lower bounds

In this appendix, we briefly review the statistical query (
{\sf SQ}
) and low-degree polynomial (
\mathsf{LDP}
) frameworks and present the proof of Theorem
1
. In particular, we provide an interpretation of our lower bounds in terms of subproblems with queries restricted to the harmonic subspace
V_{d,\ell}
.

C.1
Statistical Query lower bounds

The Statistical Query (
{\sf SQ}
) framework, introduced by Kearns
[
Kea98
]
, models algorithms that interact with data only through expectations of query functions, rather than direct access to samples. The complexity of these algorithms is measured up to some worst-case tolerance on these expectations. While based on worst-case error rather than sampling error encountered in practice, the
{\sf SQ}
framework has proven remarkably effective in analyzing the computational complexity of statistical problems, often yielding accurate predictions for algorithmic feasibility. We refer to
[
BF02
,
Rey20
]
for additional background.

Below, it will be useful to present a variant of
{\sf SQ}
algorithms, called
query-restricted statistical query algorithms
, introduced in
[
JMS24
]
. In this model, queries are restricted to a set
{\mathcal{Q}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}
of measurable functions
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. We denote
{\mathcal{Q}}
-
{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
this class of algorithms, with number of queries
q
and tolerance
\tau>0
. We will mainly consider the standard case of
unrestricted queries
, denoted
{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
, where
{\mathcal{Q}}
contains all measurable functions. In Appendix
A.3
we discuss the case of
correlation statistical queries
(
{\sf CSQ}
).

Our lower bounds hold for the detection problem (hypothesis testing) of distinguishing between

\{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}:{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\}\qquad\text{v.s.}\qquad\{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}\}.

(49)

Below, we describe
{\mathcal{Q}}
-
{\sf SQ}
algorithms in this context.

{\mathcal{Q}}
-restricted
{\sf SQ}
algorithm.

For a number of queries
q
and tolerance
\tau>0
, a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-restricted
{\sf SQ}
algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
-
{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
for detecting SIMs takes an input distribution
\mathbb{P}
from (
49
) and operates in
q
rounds where at each round
t\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
, it issues a query
\phi_{t}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, and receives a response
v_{t}
such that

\big|v_{t}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\phi_{t}(y,{\bm{x}})]\big|\leq\tau\sqrt{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(\phi_{t})},

(50)

where we set
\mathbb{P}_{0}
to be the null distribution (that is,
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
here). The query
\phi_{t}
can depend on the past responses
v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}
. After issuing
q
queries, the learner outputs
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbb{P})\in\{0,1\}
. We say that
{\mathcal{A}}
succeeds in distinguishing
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
, if
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}})=1
for all
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0})=0
.

Remark C.1
.

The variance scaling on the right-hand side in (
50
) is non-standard in the
{\sf SQ}
literature. It is introduced here as a convenient way to normalize queries, which is necessary for
\tau
to be meaningful. We note that other normalizations are possible and refer to
[
JMS24
, Remark 3.1]
for a discussion.

General lower-bound.

The following proposition is a simple, standard lower bound on the query complexity based on the second moment method (e.g., see
[
JMS24
]
):

Proposition 3

(General
{\mathcal{Q}}
-restricted
{\sf SQ}
lower bound)
.

Fix
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
. If an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}\in{{\mathcal{Q}}}\text{-}{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
succeeds at distinguishing
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
from
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
, then we must have

q/\tau^{2}\geq\left[\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}}\frac{{\rm Var}_{{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}}\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}\phi\}}{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}\{\phi\}}\right]^{-1}.

(51)

Proof.

Consider
{\mathcal{A}}\in{{\mathcal{Q}}}\text{-}{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
and denote
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
the sequence of queries issued by
{\mathcal{A}}
when it receives responses
v_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}[\phi_{t}],t\in[q]
. Here the responses are fixed and deterministic, and the queries
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[q]}
do not depend on the source distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
, and in particular
{\bm{w}}
. By union bound and Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}}\left(\exists t\in[q],\;|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}[\phi_{t}]-v_{t}|>\tau\sqrt{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}[\phi_{t}]}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{q}{\tau^{2}}\cdot\sup_{t\in[q]}\frac{{\rm Var}_{{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}}\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}\phi_{t}\}}{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}\{\phi_{t}\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{q}{\tau^{2}}\cdot\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}}\frac{{\rm Var}_{{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}}\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}\phi\}}{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}\{\phi\}}.

This implies that the
v_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}[\phi_{t}]
responses are compatible for all
q
queries with positive probability over
{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}
whenever inequality (
51
) is not satisfied, and
{\mathcal{A}}
fails the detection task in that case. This concludes the proof.
∎

The query complexity bound in Theorem
1
.(i) follows from Proposition
3
with unrestricted queries
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\sf SQ}
and the following identity:

Lemma 4
.

For
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf SQ}}
the class of unrestricted queries (all measurable functions), we have the identity

\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf SQ}}}\frac{{\rm Var}_{{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}}\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}\phi\}}{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}\{\phi\}}=\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\;\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{n_{d,\ell}}=:[\mathsf{Q}_{\star}(\nu_{d})]^{-1}.

(52)

We defer the proof of this lemma below to Section
C.1.1
. The identity (
52
) shows that the lower bound effectively decouples across the different harmonic subspaces. Below, we provide an interpretation of this result: if we restrict the queries
{\mathcal{Q}}
to be in
V_{d,\ell}
, then the
{\sf SQ}
lower bound becomes
n_{d,\ell}/\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Specifically, for each
\ell\geq 1
, define
{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf SQ},\ell}
to be the set of all queries
\phi(y,{\bm{x}})
than can be written as

\phi(y,{\bm{x}})=\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}g_{\ell}(y,r)Y_{\ell s}({\bm{z}}),

(53)

where
\{Y_{\ell s}\}_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}
is a basis of
V_{d,\ell}
. Then by Proposition
3
and the proof of Lemma
4
, we have:

Corollary 3
.

Fix
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
and
\ell\geq 1
. If an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf SQ},\ell}
-
{\sf SQ}(q,\tau)
succeeds at distinguishing
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
from
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
, then we must have

q/\tau^{2}\geq\left[\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf SQ},\ell}}\frac{{\rm Var}_{{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}}\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}\phi\}}{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}\{\phi\}}\right]^{-1}\;=\;\frac{n_{d,\ell}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

(54)

For each
\ell\geq 1
, we show algorithms with queries restricted to
V_{d,\ell}
as in (
53
) that matches this lower bound. Thus, effectively, the problem decouples into subproblems, one for each
V_{d,\ell}
: on each harmonic subspace, we have a matching upper and lower bound on the query complexity, and the optimal algorithm is obtained by choosing the optimal degree
\ell
that attains the maximum in (
52
).

C.1.1
Proof of Lemma
4

For clarity, we drop the subscript
\nu_{d}
below and denote
\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}:=\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0}:=\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
. Note that a property of the null distribution is that

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}}[\phi]\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\phi],

that is,
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is the marginal distribution of
(y,{\bm{x}})
under the uniform prior
{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}
. Thus,

{\rm Var}_{{\bm{w}}}\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}}\phi\}=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}\left[|\Delta_{\phi}({\bm{w}})|^{2}\right],\quad\text{where}\quad\Delta_{\phi}({\bm{w}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}}[\phi]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\phi].

Let’s introduce the Radon-Nikodym derivative and write

\displaystyle\Delta_{\phi}({\bm{w}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left[\left(\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{0}}(y_{0},{\bm{z}},r)-1\right)\phi(y_{0},{\bm{z}},r)\right].

Recall that the likelihood ratio decomposes into Gegenbauer polynomials as (equality in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
)

\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{0}}(y_{0},{\bm{z}},r)-1=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\xi_{d,\ell}(y_{0},r)Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)\,,\qquad\xi_{d,\ell}(y_{0},r)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{d}}[Q_{\ell}(Z)|Y=y_{0},R=r].

Similarly, we can expand
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
as

\phi(y_{0},{\bm{z}},r)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}\alpha_{\ell s}(y_{0},r)Y_{\ell s}({\bm{z}}),

where
\{Y_{\ell s}\}_{\ell\geq 0,s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}
is an orthonormal basis of spherical harmonics in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
. Using the identity
Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)=n_{d,\ell}^{-1/2}\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}Y_{\ell s}({\bm{w}})Y_{\ell s}({\bm{z}})
, we obtain the decomposition

\Delta_{\phi}({\bm{w}})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}Y_{\ell s}({\bm{w}})\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\xi_{d,\ell}(y_{0},r)\alpha_{\ell s}(y_{0},r)]}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}},

and thus,

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}[|\Delta_{\phi}({\bm{w}})|^{2}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\xi_{d,\ell}(y_{0},r)\alpha_{\ell s}(y_{0},r)]^{2}}{n_{d,\ell}}.

Denote
{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi=\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}\alpha_{\ell s}Y_{\ell s}
the projection on the degree-
\ell
harmonics. We can decompose the supremum over
\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\sf SQ}
as

\displaystyle\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\sf SQ}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}[|\Delta_{\phi}({\bm{w}})|^{2}]}{{\rm Var}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(\phi)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\sf SQ}}\frac{1}{\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{n_{d,\ell}}\left[\sum_{s\in[n_{d,\ell}]}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\xi_{d,\ell}(y_{0},r)\alpha_{\ell s}(y_{0},r)]^{2}}{\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\sf SQ}}\frac{1}{\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{n_{d,\ell}}\left[\sup_{\psi\in L^{2}(\nu_{d,Y,R})}\frac{\langle\xi_{d,\ell},\psi\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\sf SQ}}\frac{\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{n_{d,\ell}}}{\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\|{\mathsf{P}}_{\ell}\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}=\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{n_{d,\ell}},

which concludes the proof of this lemma.

C.2
Low-Degree Polynomial lower bounds

We now consider sample complexity lower bounds within the Low-Degree Polynomial (
\mathsf{LDP}
) framework, another powerful tool for studying computational hardness in statistical inference problems. We refer to
[
Hop18
,
KWB19
,
SW22
]
for background.

Below we follow the presentation of
[
DPVLB24
]
. The planted distribution with
m
samples is generated by first drawing
{\bm{w}}\sim\tau_{d}
(uniformly at random on the sphere), then sampling
m
points
(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})\sim_{iid}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}_{*}}
. The null distribution corresponds to
(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})\sim_{iid}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}
. The likelihood ratio in this model is given by

\mathcal{R}((y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})_{i\in[m]})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{w}}\left[\prod_{i\in[m]}\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})\right].

We consider the orthogonal projection
{\mathcal{P}}_{\leq D}
(in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}^{\otimes m})
) onto degree at most
D
polynomial in
{\bm{z}}_{i}
, that is, we allow arbitrary degree on the scalars
(y_{i},r_{i})
. We denote

\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}((y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})_{i\in[m]})={\mathcal{P}}_{\leq D}\mathcal{R}((y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})_{i\in[m]}).

(55)

Informally, the low-degree conjecture
[
Hop18
]
states that for
D=\omega_{d}(\log d)
:

•

Weak detection hardness:
If
\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=1+o_{d}(1)
, then no polynomial time algorithm can distinguish between
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{w}}[\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}^{\otimes m}]
and
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}^{\otimes m}
with non-vanishing probability.

•

Strong detection hardness:
If
\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=O_{d}(1)
, then polynomial time algorithm can only succeed with constant probability.

Below, we state our results for weak and strong detection for a sequence of spherical SIMs
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
with
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
. Recall that we defined:

{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})=\inf_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}

Without loss of generality, we will assume that
{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})=O_{d}(\textnormal{poly}(d))
—that is, the model can be solved in polynomial time—as stated in the following assumption:

Assumption 3
.

There exists
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the sequence
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
satisfies

{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})=O_{d}(d^{p/2})
.

We can now state our bound on the low-degree projection of the likelihood ratio in this problem.

Theorem 5
.

Let
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of spherical SIMs
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
satisfying Assumption
3
for some integer
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Consider the detection task with
m
samples as defined above. There exists a constant
c>0
that only depends on the constants in Assumption
3
such that if
D\leq cd^{2/(p+4)}
, then

\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-1\leq\sum_{s=1}^{D}\left(m\frac{D^{p/2-1}}{{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})}[e(p+1)]\right)^{s}.

(56)

In particular,

(i)

(Weak detection.)
If
m=o_{d}\left(\frac{{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})}{D^{p/2-1}}\right)
, then
\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=1+o_{d}(1)
.

(ii)

(Strong detection.)
If
m=O_{d}\left(\frac{{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})}{D^{p/2-1}}\right)
, then
\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=O_{d}(1)
.

The proof of this theorem can be found in Section
C.2.1
below.

Combining this theorem with the low-degree conjecture stated above, we conclude that no-polynomial time algorithm can detect (and thus, estimate) the spherical single-index model
\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}
unless

m\gtrsim M_{\star}(\nu_{d}).

We further remark that we recover the tight threshold
{\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})/D^{p/2-1}
from
[
DPVLB24
]
. Indeed, consider the case of Gaussian SIM with information exponent
\mathsf{k}_{\star}
. We can set
p=\mathsf{k}_{\star}
, and our bound recover the (conjectured) optimal computational-statistical trade-off
d^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2}/D^{\mathsf{k}_{\star}/2-1}
from
[
DPVLB24
]
, which matches the optimal known trade-off in tensor PCA
[
WEAM19
]
.

Decoupling across harmonic subspaces.

Again, we provide an interpretation of this lower bound as the optimal lower bound among subproblems indexed by
\ell\geq 1
. For each
\ell\geq 1
, we consider the task of detecting single-index models only using degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics. Consider polynomials that are product of degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics in
{\bm{z}}_{i}
, and denote
{\mathcal{P}}_{\leq D,\ell}
the projection onto this subspace, that is

\mathcal{R}_{\leq D,\ell_{*}}((y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})_{i\in[m]}):={\mathcal{P}}_{\leq D,\ell}\mathcal{R}((y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})_{i\in[m]})=\sum_{S\subset[m],|S|\leq\lfloor D/\ell\rfloor}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}\left[\prod_{i\in S}\xi_{d,\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}_{i}\rangle)\right].

Then we have the following upper bound on the norm of this projected likelihood ratio.

Corollary 4
.

Let
\{\nu_{d}\}_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of spherical SIMs
\nu_{d}\in\mathfrak{L}_{d}
satisfying Assumption
3
for some integer
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Consider the detection task with
m
samples as defined above and fix an integer
\ell_{*}\geq 1
. Then for all
D\geq 1
, we have

\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-1\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor D/\ell_{*}\rfloor}\left(m\frac{eD^{\ell_{*}/2-1}\|\xi_{d,\ell_{*}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{*}}}}\right)^{s}.

(57)

By analogy with the low-degree conjecture, we expect that no polynomial-time algorithm only using degree-
\ell
spherical harmonics will succeed at the detection task, unless

m\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{*}}}}{\|\xi_{d,\ell_{*}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

(58)

It would be interesting to make this subspace-restricted low-degree polynomial statement more formal, and we leave it to future work. Our harmonic tensor unfolding estimator matches this heuristic sample lower bound (
58
) for each
\ell_{*}\geq 3
.

C.2.1
Proof of Theorem
5

Recalling the expansion of the likelihood function into Gegenbauer polynomials, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}((y_{i},r_{i},{\bm{z}}_{i})_{i\in[m]})=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{P}}_{\leq D}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}\left[\prod_{i\in[m]}\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},{\bm{w}}}}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{d},0}}(y_{i},r_{i},{\bm{z}}_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{m}\leq D}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}}}\left[\prod_{i\in[m]}\xi_{d,\ell_{i}}(y_{i},r_{i})Q_{\ell_{i}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}_{i}\rangle)\right].

The norm of this projection with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\otimes m}
is then given by

\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{m}\leq D}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{i\in[m]}\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{i}}}}Q_{\ell_{i}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}\rangle)\right],

(59)

where we used

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{z}}[Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)Q_{k}(\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}\rangle)]=\frac{\delta_{\ell k}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}\rangle).

Let’s separate the zero degrees from the non-zero degrees in (
59
):

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-1=\sum_{s=1}^{D}{\binom{m}{s}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{s}\leq D\\
\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{s}\leq D\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{i\in[s]}\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{i}}}}Q_{\ell_{i}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}\rangle)\right].

To upper bound the expectation, we will not be careful and simply use Hölder’s inequality and the hypercontractivity (Lemma
20
) of Gegenbauer polynomials,

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{i\in[s]}Q_{\ell_{i}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}\rangle)\right]\leq\prod_{i\in[s]}\|Q_{\ell_{i}}\|_{L^{s}}\leq\prod_{i\in[s]}s^{\ell_{i}/2}.

We obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-1\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{D}{\binom{m}{s}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{s}\leq D\\
\ell_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{s}\leq D\end{subarray}}\;\;\prod_{i\in[s]}s^{\ell_{i}/2}\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{i}}}}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{D}{\binom{m}{s}}\rho(s,D)^{s},

where

\rho(s,D)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}s^{\ell/2}\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}.

Let’s upper bound
\rho(s,D)
. By definition
\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}/n_{d,\ell}\leq 1/{\sf M}_{\star}
for all
\ell\geq 1
(where we denote
{\sf M}_{\star}={\sf M}_{\star}(\nu_{d})
for simplicity). Furthermore, by Assumption
3
, we have
{\sf M}_{\star}\leq Cd^{p/2}
. Using
\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1
, we deduce a second upper bound

\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\leq C\frac{d^{p/2}}{{\sf M}_{\star}\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}.

Separating
\ell\leq p
and
\ell>p
, we get

\displaystyle\rho(s,D)\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\frac{s^{\ell/2}}{{\sf M}_{\star}}+C\sum_{\ell=p+1}^{D}\frac{s^{\ell/2}d^{p/2}}{{\sf M}_{\star}\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\leq\frac{s^{p/2}}{{\sf M}_{*}}\left[p+C\sum_{\ell=p+1}^{D}\frac{D^{(\ell-p)/2}d^{p/2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\right].

Using that for
\ell\leq D\leq\sqrt{d}
, we have
n_{d,\ell}\geq c{\binom{d}{\ell}}\geq c(d/\ell)^{\ell}
for some constant
c>0
, the sum simplifies to

\sum_{\ell=p+1}^{D}\frac{D^{(\ell-p)/2}d^{p/2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=p+1}^{d}\frac{D^{(\ell-p)/2}d^{p/2}\ell^{\ell/2}}{d^{\ell/2}}\leq C^{\prime}D^{p/2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{D^{2}}{d}\right)^{\ell}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{D^{p/2+2}}{d}.

Assuming that
D\leq d^{2/(p+4)}/\tilde{C}
, we deduce that

\rho(s,D)\leq\frac{s^{p/2}}{{\sf M}_{\star}}(p+1).

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-1\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{D}{\binom{m}{s}}\frac{s^{sp/2}}{{\sf M}_{\star}^{s}}(p+1)^{s}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{D}\left(\frac{mD^{p/2-1}}{{\sf M}_{\star}}[e(p+1)]\right)^{s},

which concludes the proof of Theorem
5
.

Restricted projection.

Consider the likelihood ratio projected onto product of degree-
\ell_{*}
spherical harmonics. The proof is particularly simple in this case:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\leq D,\ell_{*}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-1=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor D/\ell_{*}\rfloor}{\binom{m}{s}}\left(\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell_{*}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{*}}}}\right)^{s}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}}\left[Q_{\ell_{*}}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}^{\prime}\rangle)^{s}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor D/\ell_{*}\rfloor}\left(\frac{em}{s}\right)^{s}\left(\frac{\|\xi_{d,\ell_{*}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{*}}}}\right)^{s}s^{s\ell_{*}/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor D/\ell_{*}\rfloor}\left(m\frac{eD^{\ell_{*}/2-1}\|\xi_{d,\ell_{*}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell_{*}}}}\right)^{s},

which proves Corollary
4
.

Appendix D
Spectral estimators

In this section, we provide details for the spectral algorithm (

SP

-

Alg

) and prove Theorem
2
.

Requirement 1
.

We are going to implement our algorithms on
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
satisfying the following criteria.

1.

\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}=1
and
\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,z)\sim\nu_{d}}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}(z)]:=\beta_{d,\ell}>0\,
(w.l.o.g.).

2.

There exits
\kappa_{\ell}>1
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that, for any
p\geq 3
, we have
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\rVert_{p}\leq\kappa_{\ell}\,p^{k/2}

Note that a transformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
satisfying
2
is a special case of this requirement with
k=0
and
\beta_{d,\ell}\geq\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}/\kappa_{\ell}
, and thus the theorem will follow by invoking the guarantee for this more general
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
satisfying
1
. We first specify the spectral algorithm.

1

Input :
An example set
S=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i}):i\in[m]\}\sim_{iid}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}
, the frequency
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, and a transformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
.

Output :
An estimator
\hat{{\bm{w}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Decompose
{\bm{x}}_{i}=(r_{i},{\bm{z}}_{i})
.

if

\ell=1

then

2

Let
\hat{\bm{v}}_{m}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})\,\sqrt{d}\,{\bm{z}}_{i}.

\hat{\bm{w}}=\frac{\hat{\bm{v}}_{m}}{\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}_{m}}\rVert_{2}}

end if

3

if

\ell=2

then

4

Let
{\bm{M}}_{m}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})(d{\bm{z}}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-\mathbf{I}_{d})
.

Let
\hat{{\bm{w}}}={\bm{v}}_{1}({\bm{M}}_{m})
be the eigenvector associated with the highest magnitude eigenvalue .
end if

5

Return
\hat{{\bm{w}}}
.

Algorithm 1

A spectral algorithm on the frequency
\ell=1
and
\ell=2
.

D.1
Analysis of
\ell=2

Let
{\bm{M}}^{*}:=\mathbb{E}[{\bm{M}}_{m}]
and
(\lambda_{i}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{i}^{*})_{i\in[d]}
be eigenpairs of
{\bm{w}}^{*}
such that
|\lambda_{1}^{*}|\geq\dots\geq|\lambda_{d}^{*}|
. We first show that the top eigenvector
{\bm{v}}_{1}^{*}={\bm{w}}_{*}
with
\lambda_{1}^{*}=(1+o_{d}(1))\beta_{d,2}
and the other eigenvalues are of vanishing order relative to
\lambda_{1}^{*}
.

Lemma 5
.

We have that
{\bm{M}}^{*}
has top eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}^{*}=(1+o_{d}(1))\,\beta_{d,2}
with
{\bm{v}}_{1}({\bm{M}}^{*})={\bm{w}}_{*}
and for any
2\leq i\leq d
, we have

|\lambda_{i}^{*}|\lesssim\frac{\lambda_{1}^{*}}{d}\,.

Proof.

We have that
\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim{\sf P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)\mid{\bm{z}}]=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\beta_{d,\ell}Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)
.
We now analyze
{\bm{M}}^{*}
through its quadratic form: for any
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, consider

\displaystyle{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{M}}^{*}{\bm{w}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{(y_{i},r_{i},{\bm{z}}_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})d{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{z}}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-\mathbf{I}_{d}){\bm{w}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)(d\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{2}-1)]

\displaystyle=(1+o_{d}(1))\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)Q_{2}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)]

\displaystyle=(1+o_{d}(1))\beta_{d,2}\frac{Q_{2}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle)}{\sqrt{n_{d,2}}},

where we used the fact that
Q_{2}^{(d)}(z)=(1+o_{d}(1))(dz^{2}-1)
. As
Q_{2}^{(d)}(\cdot)
has its maximum value at
1
. Clearly, the
{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{M}}^{*}{\bm{w}}
is maximized for
{\bm{w}}={\bm{w}}_{*}
, and thus it is an eigenvector with the eigenvalue

\lambda_{1}^{*}=(1+o_{d}(1))\beta_{d,2}Q_{2}^{(d)}(1)/\sqrt{n_{d,2}}=(1+o_{d}(1))\beta_{d,2}\,.

It suffices to show that the other eigenvalues are of much lower magnitude. For any
{\bm{w}}\perp{\bm{w}}_{*}
, we have
Q_{2}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle)=Q_{2}^{(d)}(0)=(1+o_{d}(1))(-1)
, and thus, for
2\leq i\leq d
, we have

|\lambda_{i}^{*}|=(1+o_{d}(1))\frac{\beta_{d,2}}{\sqrt{n_{d,2}}}\lesssim\frac{\lambda_{1}^{*}}{d},

which concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

Our goal is to ensure that the top eigenvectors
\hat{{\bm{w}}}={\bm{v}}_{1}({\bm{M}}_{m})
and
{\bm{w}}_{*}={\bm{v}}_{1}({\bm{M}}^{*})
are close to each other, when
m
is chose sufficiently large. By Davis-Kahan’s theorem, it suffices to show the concentration between the empirically estimated matrix
{\bm{M}}_{m}
, and its expectation, in the following sense.

Lemma 6
.

There exists a constant
C>0
(only depending on
k
) such that, for any
\delta>0
, with

m\geq C\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,d}{\beta_{d,2}^{2}}\left(1+\beta_{d,2}\log^{k/2+2}\left(\frac{d}{\delta\beta^{2}_{d,2}}\right)\right),

we have that with probability
1-\delta
,

\lVert{\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*}\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\frac{\lambda_{1}^{*}}{8},

where recall that
\lambda_{1}^{*}
is the top eigenvalue of
{\bm{M}}^{*}
(see
Lemma

5
).

Proof.

Our goal is to use
Lemma

25
, with
{\bm{Y}}_{i}=\frac{1}{m}\left({\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})(d{\bm{z}}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-\mathbf{I}_{d})-{\bm{M}}^{*}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
which are zero mean, and thus,
{\bm{Y}}={\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*}
. Let us bound the each quantity of interest

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbb{E}[({\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*})^{2}\rVert_{2}=\frac{1}{m}\lVert\mathbb{E}[({\bm{M}}_{1}-{\bm{M}}^{*})^{2}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{2}{m}\lVert\mathbb{E}[{\bm{M}}_{1}^{2}]\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{m}\sup_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\,{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbb{E}[{\bm{M}}_{1}^{2}]{\bm{w}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{m}\sup_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}{\bm{w}}^{\mathsf{T}}\left[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)^{2}\left((d^{2}-2d){\bm{z}}{\bm{z}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\mathbf{I}_{d}\right)\right]{\bm{w}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{m}\sup_{{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}\left[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)^{2}((d^{2}-2d)\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{2}+1)\right].

We now note that
g({\bm{z}})=(d^{2}-2d)\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{2}+1
is a polynomial of degree 2 with
\lVert g({\bm{z}})\rVert_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}\lesssim d
. Therefore using spherical hypercontractivity (
Lemma

20
), we have
\lVert g({\bm{z}})\rVert_{L^{p}(\tau_{d})}\lesssim p\cdot d\,.
Applying
Lemma

24
then gives us

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}\left[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)^{2}\cdot((d^{2}-2d)\cdot\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{2}+1)\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim d\cdot\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\max\left(1,\log\left(\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{4}}{\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2}}\right)\right)\lesssim d\log(\kappa_{\ell})

where we used the fact that
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2}^{2}=1
and
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{4}\lesssim\log(\kappa_{\ell})
. We finally obtain that

\sigma\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}}\,.

We next analyze the other variance term using similar idea:

\displaystyle\sigma_{*}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}\left[({\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*}){\bm{v}})^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{m}\sup_{{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}\left[({\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{M}}_{1}-{\bm{M}}){\bm{v}})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{m}\sup_{{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}[({\bm{u}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{M}}_{1}{\bm{v}})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\,\frac{4}{m}\,\sup_{{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)^{2}(d\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{z}}\rangle\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle-\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\rangle)^{2}].

We would like to use
Lemma

24
to bound the expectation, for which we will first obtain a tight bound on all the moments of
g({\bm{z}}):=(d\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{z}}\rangle\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle-\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\rangle)^{2}
. Let us compute

\displaystyle\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}[d^{4}\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{4}\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{4}+1]^{1/2}=(d^{4}\mathbb{E}[\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{4}\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{4}]+1)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq(d^{4}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{8}]\mathbb{E}[\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{8}]}+1)^{1/2}=(d^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}[z_{1}^{8}]+1)^{1/2}\lesssim 1\,\,.

In the above, we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, rotational invariance of
\tau_{d}
, and
\mathbb{E}[z_{1}^{8}]\lesssim 1/d^{4}
respectively. Using hypercontractivity (
Lemma

20
), we have
\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}(\tau_{d})}\lesssim(p-1)^{2}
. We now use
Lemma

24
to conclude that

\displaystyle\sigma_{*}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}\sup_{{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)^{2}(d\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{z}}\rangle\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle-\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\rangle)^{2}]}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\max\left(1,\log(\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{4}}{\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2}})\right)}{m}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(\kappa_{\ell})}{m}}\,,

where again we used that
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{4}\lesssim\kappa_{\ell}
. Our next goal is to compute
\bar{R}=\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{i\in[n]}\lVert{\bm{Y}}_{i}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]^{1/2}
, where
{\bm{Y}}_{i}=\frac{1}{m}({\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})d{\bm{z}}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}-{\bm{M}}^{*})
. And thus,
\lVert{\bm{Y}}_{i}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}(d|{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})|+\lVert{\bm{M}}^{*}\rVert_{2})\lesssim\frac{1}{m}(d|{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})|+\beta_{d,2})\,,
using
Lemma

5
. For any
p\geq 3
, bounding the
p^{\mathrm{th}}
moment

\mathbb{E}[\lVert{\bm{Y}}_{i}\rVert_{2}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim\frac{1}{m}\left(d\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{p}+\beta_{d,2}\right)\lesssim\frac{d\,\kappa_{\ell}\,p^{k/2}}{m}\,,

Using
Lemma

26
, we have

\bar{R}=\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{i\in[m]}\lVert{\bm{Y}}_{i}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{d\cdot\kappa_{\ell}\,\log^{k/2}m}{m}\,.

The threshold for choosing
R
is:

\sigma^{1/2}\bar{R}^{1/2}+\sqrt{2}\bar{R}\lesssim\sqrt{\left(\frac{d\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\frac{d\,\kappa_{\ell}\,\log^{k/2}m}{m}}+\frac{d\,\kappa_{\ell}\,\log^{k/2}m}{m}\lesssim\frac{d\,\kappa_{\ell}\,\log^{k/2}m}{m}\,,

where in the last step, we used the fact that we are in the regime
m\geq d
, only keeping the dominant term. Therefore, for any
\delta\geq 0
we can choose some
R
that satisfies

R\lesssim\frac{d\,\kappa_{\ell}\log^{k/2}(m/\delta)}{m}\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in[m]}\lVert{\bm{Y}}_{i}\rVert_{2}\geq R)\leq\delta/2\,,

where in the last inequality, we used
Lemma

26
. Finally, we apply
Lemma

25
. With probability
1-\delta/2-de^{-t}
, we have

\lVert{\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*}\rVert_{{\rm op}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\log(\kappa_{\ell})}{m}}+t^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}}+\left(\frac{d\,\kappa_{\ell}\log^{k/2}(m/\delta)}{m}\cdot\frac{d\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}\right)^{1/3}\hskip-5.69054ptt^{2/3}+\frac{d\kappa_{\ell}\log^{k/2}(m/\delta)}{m}t\,.

Choosing
t=\log(2d/\delta)
, we obtain with probability
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\lVert{\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*}\rVert_{{\rm op}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(\kappa_{\ell})\log(d/\delta)}{m}}+\left(\frac{d^{2}\kappa_{\ell}\log\kappa_{\ell}\,\log^{k/2}(m/\delta)\log^{2}(d/\delta)}{m^{2}}\right)^{1/3}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{d\kappa_{\ell}\log^{k/2}(m/\delta)\log(d/\delta)}{m}\,.

Therefore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that, for

m_{0}=\frac{C\,\kappa_{\ell}\,d}{\beta_{d,2}^{2}}\,\left(1+\beta_{d,2}\log^{k/2+2}\left(\frac{d}{\delta\,\beta_{d,2}^{2}}\right)\right),

any
m\geq m_{0}
, with probability
1-\delta
,

\lVert{\bm{M}}_{m}-{\bm{M}}^{*}\rVert_{{\rm op}}\leq\frac{\beta_{d,2}}{16}\leq\frac{\lambda_{1}^{*}}{8},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2
: Spectral algorithm, case
\ell=2
:

For any transformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
satisfying requirement
1
, we have that choosing
\mathsf{m}
sufficiently large that is

\mathsf{m}\leq\frac{C\,\kappa_{\ell}\,d}{\beta_{d,2}^{2}}\,\left(1+\beta_{d,2}\log^{k/2+2}\left(\frac{d}{\delta\,\beta_{d,2}^{2}}\right)\right)\,

by Davis and Kahn’s theorem, we have

\min_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\lVert s{{\bm{v}}_{1}({\bm{M}}_{\mathsf{m}})}-{\bm{v}}_{1}({\bm{M}}^{*})\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{\lVert{\bm{M}}_{\mathsf{m}}-{\bm{M}}^{*}\rVert_{{\rm op}}}{|\lambda_{1}^{*}-\lambda_{2}^{*}|}\,.

According to
Lemma

5
, this corresponds to

\min_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\lVert s\hat{{\bm{w}}}-{\bm{w}}_{*}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{\lambda_{1}^{*}/8}{(1+o(1))\,\lambda_{1}^{*}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\,.

Rearranging terms, we obtain
|\langle\hat{{\bm{w}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4\,.
Finally, the above sample complexity bound of
\mathsf{m}
simplifies to the one provided in
Theorem

2
under the stronger
2
.

D.2
Analysis for
\ell=1

We now analyze the case
\ell=1
case, where the analysis is even simpler and follows by concentration of vector to its expected value. For simplicity, we will denote
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}_{1}
. Let us first evaluate the expectation of the vector statistic
{\bm{v}}_{m}
computed by the algorithm.

Lemma 7
.

We have that
\mathbb{E}[\hat{{\bm{v}}}_{m}]=\beta_{d,1}\cdot{\bm{w}}_{*}
.

Proof.

For any
i\in[d]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\bm{v}}_{m}]_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)Q_{1}^{(d)}(z_{i})]=\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)Q_{1}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\rangle)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\beta_{d,\ell}\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)Q_{1}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\rangle)]=\beta_{d,1}Q_{1}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{e}}_{i}\rangle)/\sqrt{n_{d,1}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{d,1}\cdot({\bm{w}}_{*})_{i}\,.

We conclude that
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{v}}_{m}]=\beta_{d,1}{\bm{w}}_{*}
.
∎

We now show that for sufficiently large sample size, our final estimator
\hat{{\bm{w}}}
has the desired overlap with the ground-truth
{\bm{w}}_{*}
via concentration arguments.

Lemma 8
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for any
\delta>0
and any

m\geq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}\sqrt{d}}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\log^{(k+1)/2}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{\ell}}{\delta\beta_{d,1}}\right)\right)\,\quad\text{ and }\quad m\geq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}d}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\log^{(k+1)/2}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{\ell}}{\delta\beta_{d,1}}\right)\right)\,,

respectively, with probability
1-\delta
, we have

\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}_{m},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{d^{-1/4}}{4}\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}_{m},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{1}{4}\,.

Proof.

Denote
{\bm{v}}^{*}=\mathbb{E}[{\bm{v}}_{m}]
and define
X_{i}:=\frac{1}{m}({\mathcal{T}}(y,r)\sqrt{d}\langle{\bm{z}}_{i},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle-\langle{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle)
. Calculating the variance

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]\,\,

\displaystyle\lesssim\,\,\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)^{2}d\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle^{2}]\,\,\lesssim\frac{\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m},

where in the last inequality we used
Lemma

24
and the fact that
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2}=1
and
g({\bm{z}})=d\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle^{2}
is a polynomial of degree two with
\lVert g\rVert_{2}\lesssim 1
. Moreover, for any
p\geq 2

\lVert X_{i}\rVert_{p}\leq\frac{1}{m}\left(\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})|^{p}|Q_{1}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}_{i}\rangle)|^{p}]^{1/p}+\beta_{d,1}\right)\lesssim\frac{(\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2p}\lVert Q_{1}\rVert_{2p}+\beta_{d,1})}{m}\lesssim\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,p^{(k+1)/2}}{m}\,,

where in the last inequality, we used
\langle{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}^{*}\rangle=\beta_{d,1}\leq 1
(cf.
Lemma

7
) and
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{2p}\leq\kappa_{\ell}\,(2p)^{k/2}
and
\lVert Q_{1}\rVert_{2p}\leq\sqrt{2p}
by hypercontractivity (
Lemma

20
). Applying
Lemma

27
, with probability
1-\delta
,

|\langle{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(\kappa_{\ell})\log(1/\delta)}{m}}+\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\log(1/\delta)\log^{(k+1)/2}(m/\delta)}{m}\,.

Therefore, there is a constant
C>0
such that with any
m\geq C\kappa_{\ell}\sqrt{d}/\beta_{d,1}^{2}
, with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
for small enough
c>0

|\langle{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\leq\frac{\beta_{d,1}}{4}

(60)

Now let
{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}={\bm{v}}_{m}-\langle{\bm{v}}_{m},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle{\bm{w}}_{*}
be the component of
{\bm{v}}_{m}
orthogonal to
{\bm{w}}_{*}
. Our goal is to find a high probability bound on
\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}
.
Due to spherical symmetry, w.l.o.g., fix
{\bm{w}}_{*}={\bm{e}}_{1}
and so
S\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{e}}_{1}}^{m}
, and the norm of the desired vector is given by

\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=2}^{d}({\bm{v}}_{m})_{j}^{2}}\text{ where }\,\,{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})\sqrt{d}({\bm{z}}_{i})_{-1}\,.

Observe that
{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}
is a linear combination of
m
i.i.d. vectors, however, the coefficients of linear combinations
{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})
are not independent from the vectors
({\bm{z}}_{i})_{-1}
themselves, since it is coupled with
z_{i,1}
. We will decouple the laws and make coefficients independent of the vectors. To this end, consider
(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{e}}_{1}}
and
\tilde{{\bm{z}}}\sim\tau_{d-1}
independent of
(y,r,{\bm{z}})
. Then the following two random variables have identical laws:

{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)\sqrt{d}({\bm{z}})_{-1}\equiv\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(y,r)}{\sqrt{1-z_{1}^{2}}}\sqrt{d}\tilde{{\bm{z}}}

Using such argument for each of
m
samples, and
{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}
viewed as a random vector of variables
(\sqrt{d}\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{i})_{i\in[m]}
is sub-gaussian with variance parameter

\sigma_{*}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i},r_{i})^{2}}{(1-z_{i,1}^{2})}
. Thus, with probability
1-2e^{-d}
, we have
\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}\lesssim\sigma_{*}\sqrt{d}
. Therefore, it suffices to bound
\sigma_{*}
. By
Lemma

27
, for any
\delta>0
such that
\log(1/\delta)<cd
for some
c>0
, we have with probability
1-\delta/2
,

\sigma_{*}^{2}\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{*}^{2}]+\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{\ell}\log\kappa_{\ell}}}{m^{1.5}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}+\frac{\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\log(1/\delta)\log(m/\delta)^{k}}{m^{2}}\,.

Here, the condition
\log(1/\delta)<cd
arises from the fact the function
g(z)=1/(1-z_{1}^{2})
has
\lVert g\rVert_{p}\lesssim 1
only for some
p<cd
for some universal constant
c>0
.

\sigma_{*}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}+\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{\ell}\log\kappa_{\ell}}}{m^{1.5}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}+\frac{\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\log(1/\delta)\log(m/\delta)^{k}}{m^{2}}\,.

Finally, for any
\delta<e^{-d^{c}}
, choosing sample size

m\geq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}\sqrt{d}}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\log^{(k+1)/2}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{\ell}}{\delta\beta_{d,1}}\right)\right)\,,

(61)

with probability
1-\delta/2-e^{-2d}

\displaystyle\sigma_{*}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}{C\sqrt{d}},\quad\text{ and thus, }\quad\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}\rVert\lesssim\sigma_{*}\sqrt{d}\lesssim\frac{\beta_{d,1}\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{C\sqrt{d}}}\leq\frac{\beta_{d,1}d^{1/4}}{\sqrt{C}}\,.

For
C>1
sufficiently large, we obtain with probability
1-\delta/2-e^{-2d}
,

\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}\leq\beta_{d,1}d^{1/4}

Combining this with (
60
), with probability
1-\delta/2-e^{-2d}-e^{-d^{c}}

\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq 2\beta_{d,1}d^{1/4}\,.

Therefore, we finally analyze our overlap combining with (
60
). For
C>0
sufficiently large, for any
\delta>0
, for any
m\geq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}\sqrt{d}}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\log^{(k+1)/2}\left(\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,d}{\beta_{d,1}\delta}\right)\right)\,
, with probability
1-\delta
,

\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}_{m},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle+\langle{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\beta_{d,1}-\beta_{d,1}/4}{\beta_{d,1}+2\beta_{d,1}d^{1/4}}\geq\frac{d^{-1/4}}{4}\,.

Similarly, if in Eq.(
61
), we instead chose a sample of size
m\geq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}\,d}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\log^{(k+1)/2}\left(\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,d}{\delta\,\beta_{d,1}}\right)\right)
for sufficiently large
C>1
, then we obtain a tighter control on
\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}
, and directly achieve weak recovery. With probability
1-\delta/2-e^{-2d}-e^{-d^{c}}

\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}^{\perp}\rVert\lesssim\sigma_{*}\sqrt{d}\lesssim\frac{\beta_{d,1}\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{Cd}}\leq\frac{\beta_{d,1}}{\sqrt{C}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq 2\beta_{d,1}\,.

Combining this with (
60
), with probability
1-\delta

\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}_{m},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle+\langle{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\bm{v}}_{m}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\beta_{d,1}-\beta_{d,1}/4}{\beta_{d,1}+2\beta_{d,1}}=\frac{1}{4}\,.

∎

Note that the regime of interest where we can hope to succeed in polynomial sample and runtime is when
\lVert\xi_{d,1}\rVert_{L^{2}}\gg\textnormal{poly}(d)^{-1}
i.e. which corresponds to
\beta_{d,1}\gg\textnormal{poly}(d)^{-1}
for
{\mathcal{T}}
from
1
. In this regime,
Lemma

8
establishes that with sample complexity

\mathsf{m}\leq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}\sqrt{d}}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{m}\leq\frac{C\kappa_{\ell}d}{\beta_{d,1}^{2}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\,

one can achieve the overlaps

|\langle\hat{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq d^{-1/4}/4\quad\text{ and }\quad|\langle\hat{{\bm{w}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 1/4\,.

This nearly finishes the proof of
Theorem

2
for the case
\ell=1
(under stronger
2
). Depending on the problem, the sample complexity bound either matches the one provided in
Theorem

2
, or it is suboptimal by a factor of
O(\sqrt{d})
. In the latter case, we can first get to
\Omega_{d}(d^{-1/4})
overlap, followed by another boosting phase, as long as the following assumption holds.

Assumption 4
.

There exists
\ell\geq 3
and
c>0
such that

\frac{\sqrt{d^{\ell}}}{\lVert\xi_{d,\ell}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\leq c\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\lVert\xi_{d,1}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\vee d\right)\,.

Note that this assumption holds for Gaussian SIMs according to the discussion in
Section

4
, i.e. all
\ell\equiv\mathsf{k}_{\star}\text{ mod 2}
are all optimal for samples. The next section is dedicated to showing the guarantee for boosting procedure.

D.3
Boosting the overlap to achieve weak recovery

We now show how to boost the overlap from
\Omega_{d}(d^{-1/4})
to
\Omega_{d}(1)
.
We follow the boosting procedure introduced in
[
DPVLB24
]
. The proof follows similarly, and we provide details for completeness.

1

Input :
An example set
S=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i}):i\in[m]\}\sim_{iid}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}
, the frequency
\ell\geq 3
, a transformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
, and a vector
{\bm{w}}_{0}
such that
\langle{\bm{w}}_{0},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\geq d^{-1/4}/4
.

Output :
The vector
\hat{{\bm{w}}}
.

Let
\Upsilon=\lceil\log d\rceil
be the total number of steps to be taken.

Divide the training set
S=\{S^{(t)}\}_{i\in[\Upsilon]}
into disjoint collections of
\Upsilon
steps, where
|S^{(t)}|=|S|/2^{t+2}
.

for

t=1,\dots,\Upsilon

do

2

{\bm{w}}_{t}=\mathsf{boost}\text{-}\mathsf{step}({\bm{w}}_{t-1},S^{(t)})
. end for

3

Rerurn
\hat{{\bm{w}}}={\bm{w}}_{\Upsilon}

Algorithm 2

A single step of the boosting algorithm on
\ell\geq 3
.

1

Input :
An example set
S
of size
m
and a vector
{\bm{v}}
such that
\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle=\alpha\in[d^{-1/4}/4,1/4]
.

Output :
The new vector
{\bm{v}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}}}
with
\langle{\bm{v}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle=\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}}}
.

Compute
\hat{{\bm{v}}}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}_{i}\rangle){\bm{z}}_{i}
.

Return
{\bm{v}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}}}=\frac{\hat{{\bm{v}}}}{\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}\rVert_{2}}\,.

Algorithm 3

\mathsf{boost}
-
\mathsf{step}

We have the following guarantee for one step of boosting algorithm.

Lemma 9
.

There exists a constant
C=C(k,\ell)
and
c=c(k,\ell)
such that the following holds. For the input
{\bm{v}}
such that
\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle=\alpha\in[d^{-0.25}/4,1/4]
of the
\mathsf{boost}\text{-}\mathsf{step}
procedure (Algorithm
3
), we have that for any

m\geq C\kappa_{\ell}\frac{d}{\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}\alpha^{2\ell-4}}\,,

with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
, we have
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}}=\langle{\bm{v}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\geq 2\alpha\,
.

Using this lemma we can obtain the following theorem on the performance of the boosting algorithm.

Theorem 6
.

There exists a constant
C=C(k,\ell)>1
and
c=c(k,\ell)>0
such that the following holds. On the initialization
|\langle{\bm{w}}_{0},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq d^{-1/4}/4
, the boosting algorithm (Algorithm
2
) on the training set
S
whose size is

\mathsf{m}\leq C\kappa_{\ell}\frac{\sqrt{d^{\ell}}}{\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}}\,,

with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
, we have
\langle\hat{{\bm{w}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\geq 1/4
.

Proof.

According to
Lemma

9
, choosing

|S^{(1)}|\gtrsim\frac{\kappa_{\ell}d}{\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}\alpha_{0}^{2\ell-4}}\asymp\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\sqrt{d^{\ell}}}{\beta_{{d,\ell}^{2}}}
(hiding constants in
k,\ell
), with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
we have
|\langle{\bm{w}}_{1},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|\geq 2\alpha_{0}
. The sample size threshold for subsequent iteration is strictly less than 1/2 of the previous one since
\alpha\in[d^{-1/4},1/4]
and
\ell\geq 3
. Therefore, the overlap increases geometrically, and we have
\langle{\bm{w}}_{\Upsilon},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\geq 1/4
in
\Upsilon=\log(d^{1/4})\asymp\log d
iterations. The probability of success is still
1-e^{-d^{c}}
by union bound over
\log d
for smaller
c>0
. For the total sample size it suffices to choose,

\mathsf{m}=|S|\leq\sum_{t=1}^{\Upsilon}|S^{(t)}|=|S^{(1)}|\sum_{t=1}^{\Upsilon}\frac{1}{2^{t-1}}\leq 2|S^{(1)}|\lesssim\kappa_{\ell}\frac{\sqrt{d^{\ell}}}{\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}}\,.

∎

We now return to the deferred proof that shows the overlap increases geometrically in the
\mathsf{boost}\text{-}\mathsf{step}
procedure.

Proof of
Lemma

9
.

Recall from
Appendix

B
that we use
P_{\ell}(\cdot)=Q_{\ell}(\cdot)/\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}
to denote Gegenbauer polynomial that is normalized to have
P_{\ell}(1)=1
. We first note that

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\hat{{\bm{v}}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle){\bm{z}}]=\nabla_{{\bm{v}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)]

\displaystyle=\beta_{d,\ell}\nabla_{{\bm{v}}}\mathbb{E}[Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)]

\displaystyle=\beta_{d,\ell}\nabla_{\bm{v}}P_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{v}}\rangle)=\beta_{d,\ell}P^{\prime}_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle){\bm{w}}_{*}

\displaystyle=\beta_{d,\ell}P^{\prime}_{\ell}(\alpha){\bm{w}}_{*}\,.

Consider any fixed
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and consider the following analysis. Define
X_{i}=\frac{1}{m}({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}_{i}\rangle)\langle{\bm{z}}_{i},{\bm{w}}\rangle-\langle{\bm{v}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle)
. Using
Lemma

24
, we can say that

\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]\leq\frac{2}{m^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}}}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)^{2}\,Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)^{2}\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}\rangle^{2}]\lesssim\frac{1}{m^{2}}\log^{\ell}(\kappa_{\ell})\,,

where we used the fact that
g({\bm{z}})=Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)^{2}\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}\rangle^{2}
is a polynomial of degree
2\ell
with
\lVert g\rVert_{2}\lesssim 1
. Thus, by
Lemma

20
, we have
\lVert g\rVert_{p}\lesssim p^{\ell}\,,
which allows us to use
Lemma

24
. Similarly, computing

\lVert X_{i}\rVert_{p}\lesssim\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)\,\underbrace{Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)\langle{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}\rangle}_{:=g({\bm{z}})}|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq\frac{1}{m}\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\rVert_{2p}\lVert g\rVert_{2p}\lesssim\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,p^{(k+\ell)/2}}{m}\,,

where we used the facts that
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\rVert_{2p}\lesssim\kappa_{\ell}p^{k/2}
and
g({\bm{z}})
is a polynomial of degree
\ell
with
\lVert g\rVert_{2}\lesssim 1
, and thus, by hypercontractivity, we have
\lVert g\rVert_{2p}\lesssim p^{\ell/2}
. Using
Lemma

27
, with probability
1-\delta

|\langle\hat{{\bm{v}}}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log^{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\log(1/\delta)}{m}}+\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,\log(1/\delta)\,\log^{(k+\ell)/2}(m/\delta)}{m}\,.

Therefore, invoking this guarantee for
{\bm{w}}\in\{{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{v}}\}
, we obtain that with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
,

|\langle\hat{{\bm{v}}}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle|+|\langle\hat{{\bm{v}}}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{v}}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log^{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\log(1/\delta)}{m}}+\frac{\kappa_{\ell}\,\log(1/\delta)\,\log^{(k+\ell)/2}(m/\delta)}{m}\,.

Our next goal is to bound
\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}
, where
\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}
is the component of
\hat{{\bm{v}}}
orthogonal to
\text{span}\{{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{v}}\}
. Due to rotational symmetry, w.l.o.g., let us say
{\bm{w}}_{*}={\bm{e}}_{1}
and
{\bm{v}}=\alpha{\bm{e}}_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}{\bm{e}}_{2}
. Then
\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}=\hat{{\bm{v}}}-(\hat{{\bm{v}}})_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}-(\hat{{\bm{v}}})_{2}{\bm{e}}_{2}
. So our goal is to bound

\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}\quad\text{ where }\quad\hat{{\bm{v}}}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha z_{i,1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}z_{i,2})({\bm{z}}_{i})_{3:d}\,.

Consider the following analysis similar to the proof of
Lemma

8
. For a single sample
(y,r,{\bm{z}})\sim\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{e}}_{1}}
, one can define
\tilde{{\bm{z}}}\sim S^{d-3}
independent of
(y,r,{\bm{z}})
. The following two random variables have identical distribution.

{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha z_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}z_{2})({\bm{z}}_{i})_{3:d}\equiv{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)\frac{Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha z_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}z_{2})}{\sqrt{1-z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2}}}\tilde{{\bm{z}}}\,.

Now let us define
\sqrt{d}\tilde{{\bm{z}}}\sim
consider
{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\perp}
, the component of
{\bm{z}}_{i}
that is orthogonal to . Using the identical argument from
Lemma

7
that
\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}
is sub-Gaussian in random variables
(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{i})_{i\in[m]}
, with probability
1-e^{-2d}
, we have
\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}\rVert\lesssim\sigma_{*}\sqrt{d}
, where the parameter

\sigma_{*}^{2}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})^{2}\frac{Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(\alpha z_{i,1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,z_{i,2})^{2}}{d\,(1-z_{i,1}^{2}-z_{i,2}^{2})}\,.

Using exactly the same bounding strategy used in
Lemma

8
, for any
\delta\geq d^{-d^{c}}
, we have with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
,

\sigma_{*}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\log\kappa_{\ell}}{m}+\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{\ell}\log\kappa_{\ell}}}{m^{1.5}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}+\frac{\kappa_{\ell}^{2}\log(1/\delta)\log^{k+\ell}(m/\delta)}{m^{2}}\,.

Overall, we can conclude that there exists some constant
C(k,\ell)>1
such that choosing any

m\geq C\kappa_{\ell}\,\frac{d}{\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}\alpha^{2\ell-4}},

with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
,

\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{\perp}\rVert\lesssim\sigma_{*}\sqrt{d}\lesssim\frac{\beta_{d,\ell}\alpha^{\ell-2}\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{Cd}}\leq\frac{\alpha^{\ell-2}\beta_{d,\ell}}{16}\quad\text{ and }\quad\langle\hat{{\bm{v}}}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\leq\frac{\alpha^{\ell-1}\beta_{d,\ell}}{4}\,\,.

Combining we have

\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq(1+o(1))\frac{\alpha^{\ell-2}\,\beta_{d,\ell}}{8}\,.

Finally, analyzing the quantity of desired interest under this high probability event that happens with probability
1-e^{-d^{c}}
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}}

\displaystyle=\langle{\bm{v}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textnormal{next}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle=\frac{\langle\hat{{\bm{v}}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert\hat{{\bm{v}}}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\langle{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle+\langle\hat{\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{*},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle}{\lVert{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}+\lVert\hat{\bm{v}}-{\bm{v}}^{*}\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\beta_{d,\ell}P^{\prime}_{\ell}(\alpha)-\nicefrac{{\beta_{d,\ell}\alpha^{\ell-1}}}{{100}}}{\beta_{d,\ell}P^{\prime}_{\ell}(\alpha)+\nicefrac{{\beta_{d,\ell}\alpha^{\ell-2}}}{{50}}}

\displaystyle\geq(1+o(1))\left(\frac{\alpha^{\ell-1}-\frac{\alpha^{\ell-1}}{100}}{\alpha^{\ell-1}+\frac{\alpha^{\ell-2}}{50}}\right)\geq\frac{98\alpha}{50\alpha+1}\geq 2\alpha\,.

∎

Appendix E
Online SGD estimator

In this section, we present the analysis of the online SGD on the harmonic loss which stated in
Theorem

3
.
As in
Section

3
, we will implement the algorithm on
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
for
\ell>2.

We work under the following assumption

Assumption 5
.

For each
\ell\geq 1
, we assume that there exists
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
such that
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa_{\ell}
, consider
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}:=\xi_{d,\ell}/\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}
, and we have the following inequality

\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{8}}\leq C\|\xi_{d,\ell}\|_{L^{4}}^{2},

(62)

and we denote
\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)]:=\beta_{d,\ell}>0.

We perform online stochastic gradient descent on the squared loss

L({\bm{w}};{\bm{z}}_{i},y_{i},r_{i})=\left({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y_{i},r_{i})-Q_{\ell}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}_{i}\rangle)\right)^{2}.

(63)

We consider spherical gradients and project at each step to keep
{\bm{w}}_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

1

Input :
An example set
S=\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i}):i\in[m]\}\sim_{iid}\mathbb{P}_{{\bm{w}}_{*}}
, the frequency
\ell>2
, and a transformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
, a step size
\eta
and a number of step-size.

Output :
An estimator
\hat{{\bm{w}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Decompose
{\bm{x}}_{i}=(r_{i},{\bm{z}}_{i})
.

Sample
{\bm{w}}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

for

i=1,\ldots,N

do

2

Compute
L_{i}:=L({\bm{w}}_{i-1};{\bm{z}}_{i},y_{i},r_{i})

Let
{\bm{w}}_{i}:=\frac{{\bm{w}}_{i-1}-\eta\nabla_{{\bm{w}}_{i-1}}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}L_{i}}{\|{\bm{w}}_{i-1}-\eta\nabla_{{\bm{w}}_{i-1}}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}L_{i}}\|

end for

3

Return
\hat{{\bm{w}}_{N}}
.

Algorithm 4

Online SGD algorithm on the frequency
\ell
.

We state the formal statement for weak recovery of online SGD. We focus on weak recovery, and refer to the section for explanations on how to boost the weak to strong recovery. We also only focus on
\ell\geq 3
, however notice that the proof can be adapted to the cases
\ell\in\{1,2\}.

Theorem 7

(Online SGD for learning
\nu_{d}
)
.

Let
({\bm{w}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the iterates of the SGD dynamics with the loss given by Eq.(
63
), with
b>s_{\ell}
(where
s_{\ell}
only depends on
\ell
) then conditionally on
m_{0}\geq\frac{b}{\sqrt{d}},
we then have

\tau_{1/2}^{+}\leq\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}d^{\ell-1}}{\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}},

with probability at least
c>0
for some constant
c>0.

The proof of this theorem will follow by adapting the arguments from
[
AGJ21
,
ZPVB23
]
.

The good initialization probability is at least constant (see
[
ZPVB23
, Appendix A]
). Thus, the above online SGD algorithm succeeds in total with probability at least constant bounded away from zero. We can then boost the confidence to
1-\delta
by just choosing the best estimator over multiple starts, and
O(\log(1/\delta))
trails suffice.

Let introduce some notations for the following, denote
m_{t}=\langle{\bm{w}}_{t},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle
, we define
\tau_{c}^{-}=\inf\{t\geq 0\colon m_{t}\leq c\},
and
\tau_{c}=\inf\{t\geq 0\colon m_{t}\geq c\}.

Proof of
theorem

7
.

Let
\ell\geq 3.
Consider a transformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
given by
5
.We first state a
lemma

10
on the population loss defined as

{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})=\mathbb{E}[L({\bm{w}};{\bm{z}},y,r)].

(64)

Lemma 10

(Population loss)
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
, consider the normalized tranformation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
given by Assumption
5
, we then have the following inequality

\forall m\geq 2\sqrt{\frac{s^{*}}{d}},\quad\langle\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{{\bm{w}}}{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}}),{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\leq-2(1-m^{2})\beta_{d,\ell}\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{d-1}\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle^{\ell-1},

(65)

where
s^{*}=\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-2)(\ell+d-3)}{(\ell-d/2-3)(\ell+d/2-2)}}\cos(\pi/\ell).

Discretization bounds.

In this part, we give bounds on the discretization error from the online SGD and the
population loss gradient flow. Consider the SGD iterations

{\bm{w}}_{t+1}=\frac{{\bm{w}}_{t}-\eta\nabla_{{\bm{w}}}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}L({\bm{w}}_{t};{\bm{z}}_{t},r_{t},y_{t})}{\|{\bm{w}}_{t}-\eta\nabla_{{\bm{w}}}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}L({\bm{w}}_{t};{\bm{z}}_{t},r_{t},y_{t})\|},

initialized at
{\bm{w}}_{0}
uniformly on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

Proposition 4

(Discretization bound)
.

Consider

\displaystyle p_{\eta,\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2},\mathcal{K}_{3}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{K}_{2}dT\eta^{2}}{b}+\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{2(\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}+\mathcal{K}_{2}d^{2}\eta^{2})d\eta^{2}T+2bd^{1/2}\eta(\beta_{d,\ell}+\eta d^{3/2})}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{K}_{3}Td^{1/2}\eta^{3}}{b}+\frac{\sqrt{\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{2}}T\eta^{2}d^{-1}}{b},

where we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1}

\displaystyle:=K\ell\left(\frac{4e}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\log(\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{4}^{2})^{\ell/2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{2}

\displaystyle:=4K\ell\left(\frac{4e}{4\ell}\right)^{4\ell/2}\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{4}^{4}\log\left(\frac{\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{8}^{2}}{\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{4}^{4}}\right)^{4\ell/2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{3}

\displaystyle:=2K\left(\frac{4e}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\log(\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}\|_{4}^{2})^{\ell/2}.

With probability at least
1-p_{\eta,\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2},\mathcal{K}_{3}}
, we have

m_{T}\geq\frac{m_{0}}{2}+\eta\beta_{\ell,d}\frac{\ell\cdot(\ell+d-2)}{d-1}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-m_{t}^{2})m_{t}^{\ell-1}.

Conditioned on the event
\{T\leq\tau_{1/2}^{+}\wedge\tau^{-}_{2s^{*}/\sqrt{d}}\},
we have the following inequality

m_{T}\geq\frac{s^{*}}{\sqrt{d}}+\eta\beta_{\ell,d}\frac{\ell\cdot(\ell+d-2)}{(d-1)2^{\ell+1}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}m_{t}^{\ell-1}.

Sample complexity for weak recovery

We are interested in the weak recovery setting i.e we want to bound
\tau_{1/2}
. We work under the event
\{T\leq\tau_{1/2}^{+}\wedge\tau^{-}_{2s^{*}/\sqrt{d}}\},
we then can apply the same analysis as in
[
ZPVB23
]
. We choose
\eta=c_{\ell}\beta_{\ell,d}d^{-\ell/2}
, we then have

\eta\tau_{1/2}^{+}\leq\frac{2d^{\ell/2-1}}{\beta_{\ell,d}\frac{\ell\cdot(\ell+d-2)}{(d-1)2^{\ell+1}}},

with probability at least
1-\frac{\mathcal{K}_{2}+\mathcal{K}_{3}/bd+\sqrt{\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{2}}}{b}+\exp{\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{2\beta_{d,\ell}^{2}+Cbd^{-1/2}}\right)}.

Rearranging this, it gives us

\tau_{1/2}^{+}\leq\frac{2d^{\ell-1}}{\beta_{\ell,d}^{2}c_{\ell}\frac{\ell\cdot(\ell+d-2)}{(d-1)2^{\ell+1}}}\leq\frac{2d^{\ell-1}}{\beta_{\ell,d}^{2}}\cdot\left(c_{\ell}\frac{\ell\cdot(\ell+d-2)}{(d-1)2^{\ell+1}}\right)^{-1}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
10

Proof.

We have the expansion

\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)|{\bm{z}}]=\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}\beta_{d,i}Q_{i}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle).

(66)

Consider the population mean-squared loss (we can also directly use the correlation loss)

{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})=\mathbb{E}_{(y,r,{\bm{z}})}\left[\left({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)-Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)\right)^{2}\right]=2-2\beta_{d,\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(y,r)Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)\right].

Using the above decomposition (
66
), and orthogonality of spherical harmonics

{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})=2-2\beta_{d,\ell}\mathbb{E}[Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{z}}\rangle)Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{z}}\rangle)].

(67)

Using the identity (
38
) and plugging it into (
67
), we then have

{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}})=2-2\frac{\beta_{d,\ell}}{\sqrt{n_{\ell,d}}}Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle).

Let denote
m:=\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle
, we can rewrite the loss in term of the overlap parameter. The spherical gradient of the population loss is given by

\langle\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{{\bm{w}}}{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}}),{\bm{w}}^{*}\rangle=-(1-m^{2})\ell^{\prime}(m)=-2(1-m^{2})\frac{\beta_{d,\ell}}{\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle)^{\prime}.

We can use the representation of the derivative of Gegenbauer i.e
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}({\bm{z}})^{\prime}=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)\sqrt{n_{d,\ell}}}{(d-1)\sqrt{n_{d+2,\ell-1}}}Q_{\ell-1}^{(d+2)}({\bm{z}})
(
[
ZPVB23
]
Fact C.3). So, the loss can be written as

\langle\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{{\bm{w}}}{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}}),{\bm{w}}^{*}\rangle=-(1-m^{2})\ell^{\prime}(m)=-2(1-m^{2})\frac{\beta_{d,\ell}\ell(\ell+d-2)}{(d-1)\sqrt{n_{d+2,\ell-1}}}Q_{\ell-1}^{(d+2)}(\langle{\bm{w}}_{*},{\bm{w}}\rangle).

We use the facts C.4 and C.5 from
[
ZPVB23
]
(note that the Gegenbauer polynomials in
[
ZPVB23
]
is normalized such that

P_{\ell}^{(d)}(1)=1
, meanwhile we consider
Q_{\ell}^{(d)}(1)=\sqrt{B(d,\ell)}
) to state that

\forall m\geq 2\sqrt{\frac{s^{*}}{d}},\quad\langle\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{{\bm{w}}}{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}}),{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle\leq-2(1-m^{2})\beta_{d,\ell}\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{d-1}\langle{\bm{w}},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle^{\ell-1},

(68)

where
s^{*}=\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-2)(\ell+d-3)}{(\ell-d/2-3)(\ell+d/2-2)}}\cos(\pi/\ell).

∎

Proof of Proposition
4

Proof.

In the following, we denote
r_{t}=\|{\bm{w}}_{t}-\eta\nabla_{{\bm{w}}}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}L({\bm{w}}_{t};{\bm{z}}_{t},y_{t})\|
and the martingale part
{\bm{M}}_{t}=L({\bm{w}}_{t};{\bm{z}}_{t},y_{t})-\mathbb{E}[L({\bm{w}}_{t};{\bm{z}}_{t},y_{t})].

We have the recursion

m_{t+1}=\frac{1}{r_{t}}\left(m_{t}-\eta\langle\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{{\bm{w}}}{\mathcal{L}}({\bm{w}}_{t}),{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle-\eta\langle\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{{\bm{w}}}{\bm{M}}_{t},{\bm{w}}^{*}\rangle\right).

(69)

The strategy of the proof is to use the results from
[
ZPVB23
]
